1 LADIES' LETTER

1 MT DaAa Nximix, iToques are extremely wide in front, and decors, -tad with twists and folds of contrasting velvets, a favourite combination being blue and orange. i;Poppy-red is the early autumn colour for millinery, t land is seen in chiffon for bows on hat and neck, ! in currants, cherries, and even tiny tomatoes, these i latter usually on wide•brimmed Leghorns. Entire ! amines of red velvet are so covered with block 1 'feathers and choux of chiffon that they are not too brilliant of hue.

A charming hat is of black velvet, turned up in front with a huge rosette of rose miroir velvet, centred with a paste and silver ornament, and lbehind this sweeping to the side are two tine lack plumes. r Short coats of red cloth are trimmed with black dar and adorned with military braiding, and these Viave already been worn, but principally with black skirts, and with black hats showing many plumes. For coats and capes, the leading colours 'appear to be blue in several dull shades of the gendarme order, mulberry, dark red, and a deep lawn, almost verging on beaT

A particularly handsome coat and skirt is of Venetian cloth. The skirt is plain, and the coat perfect fitting, with a prettily scalloped basques, edged with Persian lamb. The large revers are laced with some beautiful fur, or even with paltry, which will be as fashionable during the coming season as it has been for several winters past. Very narrow borderings of fur will appear on winter garments, and the frilled capes slope down In shawl fashion at the back, and the frills are Leaded by the narrow fur. Even fur capes have frills and flounces of fur, and fur ties with tails or balls have large bows of fur, which are neither graceful nor pretty, but will probably be worn as a change from the too common crossed boa.

For winter wear for a young girl coats and skirts should be made in cloth and braided with black, or gold mixed braid. A hat to wear with this should be of the Lady Blanche shape of pale grey and trimmed with fancy parrot mounts.

Chic little coats fitted to the figure, and totally distinct and in contrast to the skirt, will he a leading feature in autumn fashions. Some of the trimmings used on the3e coats are exceedingly showy, but the great consideration is to fit the figure very closely, the basque shrunk and curved until it sets without a wrinicle. A costume made with one of these coats can be made in a dark grey cloth, relieved with hip pockets, cuffs, and rever of .palest silvery grey cloth, and trimmed with a narrow band of chinchilla fur. The buttons are of cut steel and pearl, the belt of pale grey leather, and the skirt is of light grey Venetian cloth. _

, The blouse is still with us-, but dressmakers speak of it as out of date, or suggest the separate bodice as impossible. Deapil e this fact, the blouse is still .in request, and is met at smart resorts, at decidedly fashionable restaurant dinners, and is beloved of the woman who is paying a round of country house visits. True, the glaring contrast of a black skirt and distinctly separate blouse is no longer approved, but it is so easy to select a blouse in accordance with some particular skirt, and not only one, but two or three blouses, which so eclipse the ekirt that it is barely noticed. Blouses for autumn wear are in soft woollens, trimmed ruchings of ribbon, grouped lines of satin or velvet, square yokes outlined by applique motile, or insertions of lace or gimp, or are without trimmings, and elaborated by tucks and cordings. For morning wear, plaid flannel or combinations of plain and tartan veiling are useful, and all glace silks are lined with nun's veiling. Broche velvet, printed and spotted velveteen, and cashmere with printed lace designs are also employed for the übiquitous blouse. New tea gowns and tea jackets of the daintiest description are now being shown. A charming tea gown is of turquoise•blue silk, trimmed in an original way with frills of creamy lace. This gown .fs made in an entirely new style with folds of white crepe de chine in the front of the bodice and down the centre of the sleeves. The belt is of white satin finished off with rosettes of the same, and from beneath it falls a frill of lace, form. ing a short basque, which extends into waterfall frills down the skirt on either side of the front gahta kind of fichu over over the tops of the thwiedtahh.oulTdehres lanacde halaenogfaornin sleeves, which are laced over thi. crepe de chine with satin ribbons and trimmed with rosettes. "WOMEN'S CHAT."

The marriage of Princess Fedora of Meiningen,which took place at Breslan the other day,was of some interest to our Royal Family, the young bride being a grand- daughter of the Empress Frederick, and a great grand- daughter of the Queen. Her Majesty sent her youthful descendant a magnificent silver tea set, and an Indian ehawl,and was represented at the wedding by Sir Frank Lascelles.

The Emperor and Empress of Germany sent their neice a handsome dinner service, but, their Majesties did not attend the ceremony. This fact has been melon commented upon in Berlin. That no urgent business kept the Kaiser and Kaiserin away is evident, as from Selten they went, via Berlin, to the shooting castle of Rominten in East Prussia.

The younger daughter of the Empress Dowager of Rnssia, the Grand Duchess Olga, is, it is said, likely to be betrothed very shortly to her cousin, Prince Allred of Coburg. The young Grand Duchess is a splendid horse- woman,and an excellent linguist, and is a great favorite with all the members of her family. Slri is much with her brother and sister-in-law. the Czar and Czarina, and is simply adored in the Imperial nursery. There is some talk of the Grand Duchess paying a brief visit to the Queen at Windsor, during the corning winter. Apart from its adornment, a Royal bridal gown is not usually, more than ordinarily costly. It is the lace upoia it which brims the builder's account to such enormous dimensions. This in many instances has reached £2.000 for the entire dress, while in others the sum total has been larger still. When the Princess of Wales was mar- ried sho wore lace to the value of £lo,ooo—a present frcm the King of the Belgians.

Nowadays, the most that is spent by Royal brides on, boots and shoes is £5O to £lOO. The Duchess of F.fe on her marriage gave an order for three dozen pairs. For- merly it was no unusual thing for a fashionable boat- maker to receive an order for a gross of boots and shoes, and his litt'e bill usually totalled up to £250. As a matter of fact,our own princes and prince3.sea were each provided with twelve dozen pairs. The traditional idea that marriages are made,or rather consummated in the spring-time of the year,is all wrong, as statistics clearly show. It ia in the autumn,rather at the. fall of tLe leaf than at its b'rth, that the world does its marrying and giving in marriage. The season is not S 3 symbolically pretty,t he trees *recasting off their sum- mer glory, the flown have faded, and the sun has ceased to be constant, but despite all drawbacks of this description, the last three months of the year are the most favoured by those who are about to marry. In poetry it is in the spring that young men's fancies turn to thoughts of love and marriage. In fact it is the late autumn.

The ritual of the Greek 'church ordains that solemn betrothal precedes the actual marriage, in which are wed a gold :and silver wedding ring, blessed by the priest, the gold ring being given to the man, the silver ring to the woman. The form of the espousal is then repeated, and the rings are placed on the right hands, and then exchanged, that no inferiority may be be- tokened by the woman wearing the silver ring, and also to indicate a common ownership of tiroperty. The Duchess of Bedford who has just been plunged into mourning by the death of her mother, is one of our three literary Duchesses. Like the Duchess of Cleveland (the mother of Lord Rosebery) and Sutherland, she has contribute 3 many articles to various magszines. She married in 11388, and tier eldest son, Lord Tavistock, is now in his tenth year. The Duchess is an excellent shot and devoted to country puratai.

Woburn Abbey, the seat of the Duke of Bedfor3, is surrounded by a magnificent park, in which all kinds of deer, white antelopes, yaks, llamas, and even zebras are to be seen roving about, while on the lakes and ponds, any numb.r of quaint birds ab)und. The pine woods near, are exceeding picturesque, and the whole property is admirably managed under the personal supervision of the Duke. The Abbey was built in 1744 by John, Duke of Bedford, on the site of the ancient abbey. The man- sion contains a most valuable art collection, chef d'eeuvre being the ce:ehrated Bedford vase. The Bed- ford diamonds are superb,and the Duchess boasts among hrr cullection a crown of there stones, nearly two inches in height.

Castle Boro, Enniecorthy. where Lord and Lady Carew have been entertaining a large house party, is a stately home, and the gardens are josty famed for their beauty, the six terraces and three lakes being a marked feature of the pleasure grounds. These terrace l were made in order to give work to any starving labourers who came to Castle Boro, during the famine of 1798. The library with its unique collection of volumes, is an interest.ng room, for, with the quaint humor of a bygone age, the entrance to the staircase leading to the gallery has been so deftly concealei, that only those who know the secret can slide aside the panel and enter. . .

Lads' Carew, whose philanthropic work is so well known, is the possessor of the most magnificent uncut ruby in the world. The stone which weighs 130 carats, is about the size of a large walnut, and like many other rubies, is pierced from end to end, along its greatest diameter. A magnificent diamond blazes on the clasp which holds it to its chain.

Whit-e-g-jWne-ha-;e-P-;;;ed so universally becoming that even with the fear of chill October before their ' eyes, up-to date women have refuted to give them up. Muslin and prenadines have been exchanard for cloth, cashmere and serge, and instead of lace, and ribbons, narrow borderines of fur—salsle or mink tail for choice —adorn these particular frocks. White felt hats trimmed with white mirror velvet, and either white wings or ostrich feathers, are built to accompany the gowns. ' It is whispered that bonnets are coming in again arranged with strings ! This may be so, but it is not likely to be yet. The very name bonnet sounds dowdy, and suggests age. No one grows old nowadays. If we do rot wear hats we wear toques, which are infinitely more youthful in appearance and name. Modistes will have to wait until the craze for taking a quarter of a century off our age has diminished somewhat, ere they try to persuade us to don bonnets. Sacque coats are well to the fore this autumn. In many of the newest, the sleeve is carried up is a point to the neck, thus doing away with shoulder pleats alto- gether. The mode is just a little trying to all but perfect figures, still this ts not likely to prevent the novelty lover from indulging in the idea. The woman question is apparently coming to the front in Germany. At last women have wearied of their restricted lives,and are revolting against being brought up to depend entirely on the chance of marriage. They evidently intend to procure further opportunities in the way of education,and that their sphere shall be widened. Switzerland may be said to be a pioneer among Euro- pean countries, in respect of the higher education of our sex. In some of the English and Scotch medical col- leges, female students are admitted,and there are besides several university colleges for women. In Denmark,the University of Copenbagen,now more than 400 yoars old, admits women on squat terms with men, to all tranches, except theology. Women have already distinguished themselves in me- dicine, philosophy, stc., and now one of our sex, Mn.

A capital way in which to wash quilts of eider down is as follows :—Make suds of lukewarm water, and the best laundry soap. Put in the quilt and wash it thoroughly, being careful not to rub soap on it. Such a procedure causes it to shrink in spots,and is undesirable in every way. Toe best eiderdown will not impoverish and will bear hard and frequent washings. It must be well and repeatedly shaken after it is washed, the more, and oftener the better, and hung in the sun, or near a good fire, but not too near. Apple Jam.—To every six pounds of fruit allow Bye pounds of sugar, three cloves, the juice of three lemons, and a dash of cinnamon. Tare, core, and slice the aPPles and place them in the preservi g pan with the other in- gredients, and just sufficient water to keep them from burning. Boil for about an h our, stirring constantly with a wooden spoon. " MADGE." A TARPORLEY GROOIE IN TROUBLE.

To hunting people all over the kingdom, Tarporley in Cheshire, with its ancient and cdebrated hunt, is well known. The town is situated amid the prettiest pastoral scenery in tho county, almost under the shadow of the historic Beeston Castle, with Peckforton Castle, the seat of Lord Tollemsche, in close proximity. A Chester journalist, fond of rambling in the vicinity, In the course of his peregrinations, became acquainted with an affair that excited his curiosity, and forthwith made it a matter of further enquiry. The rumours all concerned a Mr. Adair, and at the White Hall, Tarporlev, the reporter found Mr. Thomas Adair, a groom in the service of ths owner of the property. He stated what bad come to his ears, and ths facts were not denied by Adair.

Asked if he would furnish further details: "Man," Adair answered, "I was just eaten up with rheumatism throughout my body. I WA.S first attacked with neur- algia at the back ot my head, and the muscles of my neck; the pain was so great that I could get no sle.p at night. That was three years ago,and since then, my joisti—knees, ankles, and elbows—have swollen to double their natural size. lam 34 years of age,married, with my wife and family in Scotland, and I was advised to go back there, but I have been in my present service twelve years, and I didn't want to abandon it."

I did so and by the time I had taken the contents of one box f found myself very much bettor."

" Well, and what then ?" " What then," he laughingly retorted. " Why I went on, of course, with another box, and by the time I had taken three boxes I found myself perfectly cured. That was a few months ago, and lam now feeling myself batter than ever I was in my life. lam certain that it was Dr. Williams' Pink Pills that cured major I took no other remedy. You may publish," be added, " what I have told you : it is all true, and thankful I am that I was led to try Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. I may tell you that my anster,when he learnt how I became cured,ex- pressed his astonishment and pleasure." Mr. William Dagnall, the coachman in the same ser- vice, st ited that he had known Mr. Adair over twelve years, who, until his recent illness, always had good health. "I want compensation for the loss of two hundred and seventy head of cattle." "The Mexican law-courts are open to those who desire litigation," Muro significantly replied, "but the process is a costly one. If you start legal proceedings with a shirt on, you may be minus one in the end. Law is an expensive luxury, and often unsatisfactory even to the successful client, when he sees his bill of costs. Ah ! a happy thought! Get the beautiful Lucia Diaz to be your Portia." Mark gave a gesture of indignation, for the measured insult stung him. "Take my advice," sneered Muro, "play the chicken and shelter yourself under the American wing. There are hawks about, and fledglings are not safe alone. Thank your lucky stars that I gave my word of honour for your safe escort; but it did not include your impudent, red-haired little Scotch- man. Californian sentiment is against foreigners. and you both share the odium. Go!" "Foreigners!" reiterated Mark, fearlessly folding his arms upon his broad chest, and scornfully eyeing the freebooter. "The lawless appear to be first favourites!"

Muro laughed ironically. My heart seemed to stop with dread as a slight scuffle in the inner room and a gentle shaking of the curtains across the open door warned me of danger. Muro's hand was upon the hilt of his knife. I watched him with the eyes of a falcon, and a feint even had roused the worst part of my nature. I would he shot the rascal without the least compunction had he attacked Mark Pepys. I touched my friend's arm, and the wrathful intent on his face relaxed immediately. He wheeled round and winked at me, which meant waiting his time for retaliation.

"Foes lurk behind the curtain" I whispered. Mark defiantly looked over his shoulder, and Muro bowed with the dignified grace of a medieval courtier. Then arm-in-arm we strolled out, dis- gusted with the buffoonery of Mexican justice. As we passed through the camp Mark's lip were com- pressed and his cheeks flushed. His high reputa- tion and courage alone saved him from the soldiers' insults and gibes. Our Indian retainer, Pecos the hunchback, awaited us as though we were recovered from the jaws of ,10th. "Senorita Lucia Diaz," said he, plaintively, "hath sent to warn thee of danger. Sh• says the eagle is preparing to worry the lion." "We shall not be kicked out like dogs with the mange," replied Mark defiantly. "Muro has thrown down the glove. I accept the challenge." A "pard " handed him a printed paper, and as he read it he laughed in his sunny-hearted way. "A Mexican proclamation, calling on all honest citizens to exterminate us. This is sooner said than done. Sandy, whip the lads together; scour the country for volunteers. We will fight to extremity, refuse quarter, and leave the British- speaking races to avenge our deaths!" The clash of arms and the clarion note of battle were to stir the settlers against Mexican perfidy. Muro, quick-witted and fiery, endeavoured to take time by the forelock; but within a week we had gathered a motley band of men, who were, perhaps, better pioneers than colonists, of roving dis- position, plucky at close quarters, smart as sharp- shooters, and experienced at "brushes" with Indians. But Muro's unmitigated ruffians knew the trails, and were comparatively safe among the rugged mountain!, of a sparsely-populated country. One terribly dark night our bivouac was in a dense wood. A hurricane of rain put out our tires. I went the rounds at midnight, drenched to the skin, and aching in every limb. Mark's turn was at cock-crow. On relieving guard a Cali- fornian volunteered to remain until three a. m., as lie felt quite wideawake and able to bear the strain. Praising his self-sacrifice, I returned to my tent. We were all dead beat, and sleep came tP our relief. Our patrols were exhausted, and their endurance severely tested ; for sleepless nights and hazardous days will try the most faith- ful. Some slept at their posts ; but the volunteer watchman kept his vigil with uncommon pluck. So well did the treacherous ally fulfil his promise that he betrayed us into the hands of Mum. Mark and I were overpowered as we slept, and the whole camp surprised with such subtlety that the faintest resistance was a mad bid for death —an ignominious hanging by the neck, without even the merciful drop and dislocation. All the lads were disarmed, and, having received a caution that if they opposed Moro again they would be summarily dealt with, were dismissed. Mark and I were marched prisoners into the enemy's camp, amid the triumphant shouts of a rapacious horde, half savages, and notoriously cruel. We were to be sport for these merciless raiders, and we reckoned that nothing short of a miracle could save us from a lingering death.

SECRET OF MARK PEPYS.

Br FRED J. PROCTOR, Author of "Timothy Twill's Secret," "Jean

Marlou," 'q:Or. Dobbs," to.

CHAPTER I. STRIKINO THE EXTICOTIL

My long residence as a mountaineer In the wilder districts of California, where the interest of this narrative is centred, is a guarantee that I wish to draw upon my memory rather than my imagina- tion. My story concerns the exploits of say comrade and employer, Mark Pepys, a giant among us Britishers, generous, good-tempered, fearless, and brave as a young lion. Whence he came, why, how he obtained his money, and his upbringing, were questions of speculation until a pretty woman, named Lucia Din; began to win his love and entice his confidence.

There was a charm of romance about the desire of Lucia to effect a conquest of Mark's heart. The couple had met at dances prior to the estrangement between the Mexicans and Saxons—brought about by the evident plan of the American troops to annex California. The frankness of Mark's character, his robust health, his dark laughing eyes, his fine physique and firmly-chiselled features, and, above all, the ardent nature of his wooing, enthralled the lovely Senorita—the daughter of a deceased Castilian grandee, proud of her lineage, and fond of the environment of Western pastoral life, with its gaiety, chivalry and adventure.

At first their meetings were casual and accidental, but the observant soon noticed the increasing regularity of their courting, and that they enjoyed each other's society and basked in the glamour of love's glowing sunshine. The warmth of Lucia's impulse strengthened her desire to win the man whose gallantry inspired her passion with poetic enchantment. It was the old, old story remodelled. The finest Saxon of those parts had been attracted by the loveliness, the naivete, the culture, of a graceful Spaniard. Her melting, languorous eyes shone with all the intensity of young love. He had never kissed such lips, he had never smoothed such glossy hair, whose seducing folds fell in artistic plaits and ringlets upon the graceful curves of her snowy bosom. He was to be forgiven if haunted by her merry ripple of laughter, her sweet, musical voice, and a coquetry as magnetic as it was race-given, to win the brave and subdue the strong. To him her cheeks were as soft as velvet, inspired by the colour of a delicate rose leaf, clear and more beautiful in the shade of her lace mantilla. The long llshes of her full eyelids gave a becoming tone to her dark eyes; her intelligence and ladylike demeanour filled him with an irre- sistible desire to call her his adored one, his own true love.

Her bide feet in pretty slippers, and her slender ankles, chimed in, so to speak, with the exquisite symmetry of her graceful waist and small tapering fingers. Supple movements when riding or walking, queenly courtesy at receptions, winsome smiles and fascinating conversation, seemed to bewitch Mark Pepys. Her singing was a revelation; but—but Lucia had a temper, and, moreover, she was of a fearfully jealous disposition. At least, this was my estimate of her, when I thought the tenderness of her passion might ruin my chief ; for though his patrician courtesy won the praise of some wealthy girls, and several mothers angled dexterously on behalf of their daughters, yet his general bearing to the fair sex seemed hitherto to indicate his aversion to marriage. In this latter decision I heartily acquiesced, for I was myself not by any means a marrying man. So that when others welcomed the apparent weaving of these two young hearts I chortled, with an unmistakable sneer upon my lips. When gossips said that Manuel Muro was also enraptured of Lucia's divine beauty my melancholy fears tickled Mark's fancy, and, with his face exultant, he quickly replied: "Sandy Dees, mind your own affairs." The fear of him dropping from the sublime dreamland of love to the ridiculous position of a lover jilted and humiliated must be my excuse for interfering. Yet, If Manuel Muro also admired Lucia's loveliness, things might be a trifle warm in our quarter. At any rate, I can honestly say that my solicitude was solely on Mark's behalf, for, if Lucia had not been spoilt by fulsome flattery lavishly bestowed by many, then she was a jewel of a girl! Now, about the time when this young lady set herself cleverly to influence the, character and shape the welfare of my friend, a trivial incident occurred which whetted my curiosity and aroused my suspicion about the mystery of Mark Pepys's earlier years. The starting point was simply this. At the cattle-branding Mark's sleeves were tucked up, and I saw a singular device tattooed on his brawny left arm. Of course, tattooed skin was nothing strange, for in the days before the gold- seekers swarmed the hills like myriads of busy ants and changed the whole aspect of nature most of us were tattooed, to assist identification in case of unnatural death. But the design on Mark's arm was classical. The top third of a shield was dotted, to represent gold or yellow in heraldry, with a bird on either side of a rose. The remaining apace represented black or sable, with a white or argent cross, decorated with a small lion rampant and four feline heads.

This design gave Mark an idea. Finding that an ordinary brand was little protection against cattle- sifting, he had this device forged in rough outline for a branding-iron, and prior to using it he took me aside and flourished the new iron with a good deal of pride. "Sandy," said he frankly, and with determina- tion, "I'll set this brand red-hot upon Manuel Muro's scowling brow the first time I catch him stealing my cattle. I'll cloud the fire in his dark, resentful eyes! " "A new brand!" I exclaimed, wagging my head, and then fixing my puzzled gaze upon his firmly modelled face. "A family crest or college cost-of-

He laughed at me most heartily, and answered: "It brings back the memory of romps I had in the hay-field, and the pleasure of winning prizes for wrestling, boxing, and single-sticks." "At what public school did ye fag?" I asked, far too eagerly. His frown added importance to the expressive tone of his reply: "You have an inquisitive mind, Sandy, and a nose prone to poke itself into other people's business. Don't ferret in my copse, and we shall be friends. This is my adopted country; here I shall form new ties and forget the past." "I see ye're a'ready head o'er ears i' love wi' Lucia Diaz."

"The prettiest girl," he interposed with warm admiration, "that ever put dainty foot in slipper— s girl who has brought the first rays of sunshine into my life for many a long day—a gentlewoman whose refinement and intelligence have won her great popularity among the Mexican forces—a charming singer, always merry. What do you think of ray choice? " "To look at the match free a selfish standpoint," I replied consolingly, "it is somewhat a guarantee •fer our safety against Muro's intrigues." He shrugged his broad shoulders, and with a half-savage gesture slung a tuft of grass at a mustang that madly careered about us. 'then over his clean-shaven face stole an anxious look, as he thoughtfully added: "Come with me to Muro. Father Otando saw one of his men at Vespers, dis- guised as a beggar. Our lads discovered the coon sneaking off under cover of the Presidio alcoves, and kicked him off the premises. The same night my cattle were stolen." "Juist what I expeelnliv" said I, with a full- flavoured twang. "Thank gniektess I've naught, so the de'il canna draw my .Ug. llarkye, it's dangerous to enter Muro's gimp." "Bahl his rapscallions are of the lowest type, nicknamed troops. They possess neither a service- able jacket nor a sound pantaloon among them. Look you, Sandy, if Muro throws sandwiches at my head I shall reply with brickbats. His half- castes are rigged out in squalid shreds; their blankets have not seen soap for an age. Troops, ha! hal my shoeblacks await them." Mark ran his fingers through his curly brown hair, leisurely lit a cigar, and walked away with an air of easy dignity. After some irksome formalities we rode over to Muro's camp, and were ushered into the commander's presence. He was reclining in an easy-chair, smoking a cigarette. He pointed to a couch, but we elected to stand. He raised his eyebrows with an expression of impatience, and Mark haughtily detailed the circumstances of the lifting of his cattle. Muro smiled, and said: "If my men go raiding and fall into a hornet's nest they must not grumble if they are stung," and, after burking the ques-

CHAPTER 11.

THE ROYAL CROSS.

Now, we soon discovered that many of Muro's men were emaciated by a disease resembling leprosy. Two friars and a nun from the nearest mission were alleviating suffering to the best of their ability. Lucia Diaz nursed Muro, whose sickness was the reason why we were not shot forthwith. The ravages of the plague increased daily, and a special horse and cart was placed at the friars' dis- posal for the removal of the dead by night. It transpired that the missioners were on confi- dential terms with Lucia Diaz, who espoused our cause, and contrived the setting us at liberty. "When Muro grows stronger," Lucia argued, "his compassionate mood may vanish. Of the two friars one is short and stout. Sandy Dees must make up to resemble him; while you, Mark Pepys, must assume the role of the taller one. I will supply the necessary things. and accompany you, dressed as a nun. We will bury a dummy body, the impromptu hearse being driven by a confidential teamster. As everyone loathes the disease, not even a patrol will approach the red lights we shall carry. This scheme is the safest I can devise." And then I saw that the Senorita's admiration for Mark Pepys had ripened into a passionate and avowed love, for she elected to flee into Yerba Buena—Mark's coast quarters, near the now cele- brated "Golden Gate." Accordingly, a dark night was selected for this hazardous feat, and the amateur burial party connived at imitation so thoroughly that the little procession struck terror into the hearts of the curious. The risk was too great for comedy; and yet I smiled when Mark whispered: "Sandy, you look a veritable Father Pelican—podgy of trunk, though lean of visage." As the rough hearse and sham priests passed Out of the camp, Lucia was haunted with the fear of discovery; but she played her part with consummate skill. The whole trick was carried out with such cautious bribery and simplicity of action that we wended our way to the consecrated ground with- out molestation or suspicion. Then we sought and found our tethered steeds, put the lights out, mounted speedily, and sped away north-west into the pitch darkness of a dense forest, trusting to the sagacity of our trained horses and the piloting of our teamster guide. "When the earliest birds herald the break of day," observed Lucia, "Muro's anger will be as a consuming fire."

For my own credit's sake, and in view of thrill- ing continkencies, I am bound to state that I never guessed the extent of Lucia's sacrifice, nor the depth of her love. As for the brigand, when he heard of our escape with Lucia's connivance, he laughed merrily ; and sent a special carrier with ,ifts for the Befiorita, a snip of cassia bark for repys, and a bit of clean slate for his "henchman" —meaning your humble servant, Sandy Dees. "Muro bids for peace," observed Mark; "Lucia Diaz advises it, so I have agreed to a truce."

"Gang alang wi' ye! I'm no' yet bereft o' my senses. He'll pluck oot as thou hairs o' yer heid as it thkes bees to mak' a honeycomb." Nevertheless, "Mercury " was feasted and sent back to Mum, Lucia Diaz giving the message, and guaranteeing us freedom and immunity from attack. Mark had implicit faith in her assurances, though how she could answer for our safety was a mystery, especially to an incredulous man like myself. "When Muro cries fer peace," said I cynically, "he sharpens his sword in secret. When that sword is drawn t' stab in the dark, we'll hae (Air wark cut oot t' blunt it."

I was not surprised, therefore, when at Easter- tide the cunning freebooter sailed up the Pacific coast, and anchored MT Yerba Buena, flying Mexican colours. Mark was puzzled, and Lucia agitated, for Muro came ashore in quite a free and easy style, and without escort. Our attitude was one of armed neutrality. One afternoon Pecos rowed me and our dog Jip at a safe distance. round the suspicious schooner,

My manner indicated mingled wonder and anxiety as I observed: "What! dost thou say the villain attempted to lay on the thong—to strike Lucia Diaz, whose beauty curbs the rascals and keeps the fools in leading-strings ? " "Nay; the elk threatened in his fury, but the lion seized him by the throat. The Senorita staggered with fright, and fell in a swoon. There- upon the White Chief sprang to her side, and tenderly lifted her, the while her teeth chattered like castanets. My lord shook his fist at the gitano, who sprang upon his mustang and galloped off—his face wicked, his eyes wild with a desire for revenge. I told Harry Puke, who laughed, Baying: ' Two fine dogs snarling over a piece of meat. AM Mark Pepys is not safe in America, and he dare not go home.' "

I pointed the stem of my pipe at Pecos and shook my head, for I could not credit the jolly Boniface of Puke's Delight with such injudicious words. Pecos took an Indian charm from his breast, and pressed it to his brow—a sign of truth- fulness peculiar to his tribe. "In a swift, rapid stream," he said, "no sane Indian swims out his depth." "Thy heart wills further, Pecos, but thy lips close. Jip is dumb!" The Indian drew out a golden cross set with precious stones, which be addressed half-sorrow- fully : "Thou bast seared my brain as thou dost burn my fingers. Take the accursed thing, Sandy. I found it where the Senorita fell. None of lower estate than Queen Kane dare wear such a cross." I examined it carefully, noting the goldsmith's skill, and judged its worth to be great. I said I would restore it to Lucia at a convenient time, and told Pecos that prudence dictated the strictest secrecy. "Sandy, my lips are sealed as the mouth of Kana's Royal sepulchre, so that none, save the Princess Olive, know the hidden secrets." lie put the nails of his two thumbs upon his closed lips, and bowed reverentially. I was puzzled, for Muro's threatening attitude might be a subterfuge to embroil Mark. "Pull for the shore, Pecos. Thou art a good fellow." Judicious praise cheers the heart, and the Indian pulled with a will. As we approached I saw Lucia Diaz standing on the beach, and towards her walked Mark Pepys, of whom the jolly tavern- keeper had uttered words laden ..with ominous meaning—words which proved the significance of Mnro's innuendo.

Now Pecos forgot to feather an oar, and catching "crab," fell over the seat, much to Lucia's amusement. However, almost immediately the boat grounded, and Pecos was soon ashore hauling at the painter. Jip bounded out, frisking about Mark with delight. I jauntily followed, and doffed my hat to the lady. "Sandy," said my chief in an undertone, "I am taking the Senorita to the dance." My jaw fell, and he whispered: "The store-room is locked and barred, and the treasure is perfectly safe. Good- bye for the present." With that they left, and I lingered along the shore for half an hour or so. My mind WAS racked with ugly forebodings, and as I wended my way in gloomy silence Pecos came within talking range before his stealthy movements broke upon my reverie. I started at the sound of his voice, clenched my fists and closed my teeth. "Sandy," said he, "I crave thine ear." Not his words but his manner impressed me. "Fool!" I cried in Indian, half snarling. "For the moment I thought it was some wild animal stealing upon me! ' And I loosely beat the air with my fingers. "The bravest quail if suddenly alarmed," he answered, with quite a philosophical air, as he noticed my blanched face. "I warn thee, Sandy, some plot thickens. The Senorita hath slipped from the sward, and talks with Muro in yonder bridle path." "Spying is no sin when danger threatens. Take Jip to the tavern and there abide." And, seeing that I was in a serious mood, Pecos went with characteristic obedience, Jip joining him at my bidding, his waving tail and bright eyes expressive of pleasure. I was dubious, and tried in vain to gauge Lucia's policy. I plunged into a bed of "sweet herb "—from which Yerba Buena received its name—and then stood and argued whether it would be best to beat round by the bleak sand-hills, or to dodge among the adobe blocks and tents. The aroma from the shrubs was refreshing; the waters of the glorious bay sparkled before me, and the pleasant-looking hills of Alameda gave the scene a picturesque aspect. But the small cove with the shelving beach was deserted, and not even a fisherman moved among the rocks or lounged upon the Punta Bonita smoking. If Muro and Lucia plotted they had a fair field. If they harboured sinister designs it was my bounden duty to upset them. I peeped into the modest church which glistened in the bright sunlight, but not a soul prayed. To avoid detection I slipped among the irregularly- grouped houses, and soon came upon the earliest Presbyterian tent. The place was closed. I now hesitated in my task, but unpleasant reflections urged me forward. ‘Ah I yon tent set doon haphazard amang that heavy chapparal is the hame o Lucia Diaz. She an' Muro may hae made fer this tent." A tiny Mexican flag flew at the top of a slender pine-pole at the gate. Approaching the door, : wondered if some plot were hatching within, and I gently called. No reply came, and I was mean enough to peep in. It was furnished with Eastern magnificence, the things arranged in the very acme of good taste. All was silent, and so, should I desist No, my mind was bent on ferreting out the couple, and yet I felt the blood rise to my neck and face with shame; for if Lucia discovered me she would report me as a scheming huckster, a trickish fellow who had stooped to play the spy. But the thin, incisive voice of :Pecos rang in my ears: "The plot thickens," and I gave a shivering sigh. I, therefore, started again on my quest in a rather unpleasant frame of mind, and traced the Iregular path through the bush and sand-hills, past ' the shanty where Pecos lived. I certainly glanced at this wooden shell, but having no peculiar interest excited by the appearance of the place, I jumped the gully, avoided the dangerous parts, shored with logs cut from the surrounding hills, and mentally thanked Pecos for strewing brush- wood over the quagmires. And as I pressed onward my blood warmed to the work, quelling my doubts. I jumped over coffee-boxes, tea-chests, battered tins, and all the paraphernalia of this refuse heap of primitive Yerba Buena; for this was before the rush for gold gave a fillip to trade and caused San Francisco to spring up with astonish- ing rapidity. Anon I stood to listen. Once I climbed a small pine tree and peered about, to observe the plot thickening and the culprits secretly planning their nefarious schemes. I even wondered if there was a conspiracy on foot to carry off Mark Pepys to the Sierra Madre and demand a ransom for his release. I grew more excited, and imagined all sorts of ridiculous things, exaggerating as a school- boy does when his imagination turns on brigands. The fact was, I hated myself for spying on Lucia—it seemed so mean and detestable. And yet I was spurred on to the work by some queer notion of danger threatening my friend, a danger I was con- strained to avert. So I fought through thorns and tangled shrubs, stumbling over rotten logs, splash- ing myself in gullies, bruising my arms and knock- ing my kneecap in my unwarranted haste. And for what? Only to stumble headlong into a path where Muro and Lucia were in close conversation! He laughed at my discomfiture, and made some ribald jest. Lucia joined in the merriment, saying: "What a pickle for a spy!" "The little Scotchman looks like some drunken clown thrown out of an inn," sneered the braggart, coming forward. "To dub him a snake would be an insult to the reptile world. A contemptible spy is beneath kicking!" As Lucia pushed the infuriated Castilian back he spat upon the ground in sheer disgust. I writhed under such insults, for I had sprained my ankle and cricked my back. She whispered to my tormentor as he was about to come at me, and he instantly swaggered down the path. But 1 savi by the contortions of his body how ill-satisfied ha was, and was not surprised when he wheeled round. His glimmering white face and threatening attitude were not reassuring. It was too late for me to repent. I must brave it out. So I fought with pain, stifled a groan, and faced him, saying: This is a conspiracy." "Let me cut a twig to thrash the toad," he sniggered malieinesly. Lucia seized his wrist, restraining me by a gesture. "Let him be," she jeered; "he is ashamed of himself." _ _

The new Lord Curzon is the second of that name of which the country can at recent boast, but the title of the older Lord Curzon is a mere courtesy title worn by the eldest son of the Earl of Howe, who is member for one of the divisions of Bucics. That Mr. Curzon, in accepting the title, should have retained his family name, has caused some surmise when, as most people say, be might as well have been called Baron Kedleston as Baron Curzon. Being an Irish peerage, it does not carry with it a seat in the House of Lords unless its owner should I be elected one of the Irish representative peers, net does the title deprive its owner of his right to sit in the Houseof Commons. Both the Lord Curzons are the eldest eons of peers, and in the course of nature may be expected to find themselves called to the Upper Chamber. Whoever has been ingenious enough to think of making Mr. Curzon's peerage an Irish one has conserved his opportunity of a career in the House of Commons, if so great a being as an Indian Viceroy can condescend to sit in the Lower House. At the same time. no Irish peerage has been created for the last 30 years.

I learn that the Admiralty authorities are greatly gratified with a:return which has just been prepared in connection with the shooting tests of the Jest six months. In the view of the experts it estab- lished the fact that our sailor a are the best naval marksmen in Europe. It may be remembered that both our sailors and marines are in the habit of shooting at a target 10ft. by sft, at ranges varying from eighteen hundred to three thousand yards, ships stearr ing the while at the rate of ten to twelve knots an hour. It is stated that the results which accomplished are remarkable, and place supremacy of ths British navy beyond haveathlledobuebetn. shooting

--- The advances in the technique of photography have made the science of so much value in the study 07 medicine that the time may come when the medical student will tind that another burden has upon him and that he is called upon to iners this special subject. At present h otographY sbaeteinsrlyaied xfun is merely pursued as a "hobby,o and those who take it up for the most part do so for pleasure. Meanwhile, in view of its great understand, in medical science, some- thing value, e, os might be advantageously done to stimulate its pursuit among those intending to follow a medical career. The College of Preceptors, for ex- ample, could readily include it among the optional bjc in connection with their examination for the registration of medical students. This would really be an arrangement which could not fail to service and would entail no hardship upon y students. The optional subjects at Present prove suggested, such as natural philosophy, optics, and similar subjects, are not those which are usually studied for their own sake, nor can they be said to have much educational value for those about to em- bark upon the study of medicine. With photography, however, the matter is quite different. Equipped with a serviceable knowledge of the latter, a stud- ent soon begins to find a valuable field for the exer- cise of his skill while prosecuting his medical studies, and I am assured that in the present day a student who proves himself a good photographer has frequent opportunities of proving himself of use to his teachers.

-- News 4f Sir Arthur Ford's death will be re- ceived with regret by all politicians without dis- tinction of party. As Secretary to the Admiralty 1 he was a painstaking and well-meaning member of Lord Salisbury's second Administration although: his classification scheme was certainly not popular among the dockyard men. He will be,f should say, ' best remembered by his association with workmen's , coupensation legislation, for he introduced a 'measure which was the forerunner of last year's Act, and was indeed wider in its application, be- cause the definition workman included every rail- way servant, every person employed in or alma a public conveyance by land or in or about a vessel engaged in inland navigation, every person serving on board a British ship, either in the capacity of master, officer, engineer,purser,or chief steward,and every person employed as a domestic or menial servant. This measure was backed by Mr. Matthews (now Lord Llandaff), Mr. Chamberlain, Sir E. Hill, Mr. (now Sir Elliott) Lees. and Mr. Tomlinson, but, though it was laid before Parliament, no opportu- nity for discussing it could be found. Last session Sir Arthur brought in a Bill to abolish the defence of common employment in cases of injuries to workmen arising in occupations not within the Compensation Act of last year,and very nearly sac- ceeded in getting it through committee.

I am moved to revert to the vaccination question because of the proceedings before the Cheltenham bench, in which the magistrates demanded, and ell but one of the applicants refused to pay, a fee of is. 63. for a conscientious objection certificate. I cannot see the slightest justification for this de- mand. When in Grand Committee on the Vaccina- tion Bill Sir Walter Poster proposed his amendment providing for the issue of a certificate of exemption, on the making of a statutory declaration, there was mention of a fee to be paid if the person registering required a certificate. But when the clause was submitted to the House by Sir Walter Poster in the amended form in which we know it, there was no I mention of a fee at all ; nor is there in Mr. Chap- lin's clause. On another point the bench is reported to have decided that every application shall be made personally by the father of the child, or, where the father is dead, by the guardian, &c.Now, 'here is what took place in the House on July 25. 'After Mr. Chaplain's clause had been added to the I Bill, Mr. Labouehere asked, inter ails, whether the obligation to satisfy two magistrates meant that the objection must be undertaken by the father, or that it might be fulfilled either by father or mother; and whether, if by the father alone, a child whose father was absent from lime during the period allowed for this formality could be contracted out of the obligation to be vaccinated by the mother appearing before two magistrates and stating her conscientious objection. Mr. Chaplain replied that the obligation should be undertaken by the person in custody of the child, whether father or mother. If the father was prevented by absence or other unavoidable cause, then the duty would naturally fall on the mother.

It is somewhat singular that under the new rules of procedure, no matter which party may happen to be in office, the average length of the daily sittings of the House of Commons Moms very little variation. According to an analysis of the figures relating to the past session, just prepared by Mr. W. 14. Ley, the Clerk of the Journals, the total number of hours during which the House this year at was 996, and the average length of sitting daily eight hours 2247 minutes.. In 1897 the average was eight hours 8 44-IS7 minutes, whilst—to take the two full sessions of the last. Liberal Administra- tion —it was eight hours 27 175-226 minutes in 1893-4. and eight hours 25 3-113 minutes in 1894. In thiQ connection the following entry from the diary of Lord Colchester is well worth recalling : " 1808 —Monday, July 4. Prorogation. The most laborious session for hours of sitting ever known 1 within living memory of the oldest members or officers of the T-Tonse. There were 115 sitting days. amounting to 829 hours, averaging 7; hours a day."

I hear that the Anarchist leaders at present in London have been deeply impressed by the tend- ency of public opinion. As the result of an ex- change of views, they have resolved to ad andon for a while all agitation, and will relapse into mere ordinary citizens, renouncing even the machinery of the Anarchist movement. So resolute are they 1 in this determination that they have even, so I am informed, resolved to abandon the steps decided on for recalling into active existence the branch organisations in the provinces. This policy is not so much the result of internal weakness and dis- putes as it is due to the desire to provide no excuse for the suggestion that they have abused the hos- pitality of this country. The matter has been ade- quately discussed, and that the wisest plan for the naoment is to remain absolutely quiet.

Joseph and Anne Sherwood. husband and wife. and Alfred Murray. were on Tuesd .3, committed by the Liverpool stipendiary for trial at the sessions on a charge of using the house. 75, Brisbane-street. for the purpose of betting. Alice Marsden was also committed, as occupier of the house, on a charge of permitting it to be so used.

At Wednesbury on Tuesday the Darlaston Galvanising Hollow Ware Company were fined £4 for neglecting to report °nee of lead poirooing to the Home Office.

On Tuesday afternoon a young heifer, when nearing Pais- ley railway station, became excited and took possession of the central platforms, quickly clearing them of passengers and officials by its ruslps. For half an hour it defied all attempts to capture it. four men being injured by its charges. DurinZ this time the passenger trains from Glasgow had to be stopped outside the station and the platforms barricaded until the passengers got out. The beast was eventually secured after four bullets had been shot into it.

A tow paper hanging machine originating in the United States is provided with a rod on which the roll of paper is placed. There is also a paste receptacle at the back, which applies the paste automatically as the paper is unrolled. After the end ol the paper is once set at the bottom of the wall, and the machine started on the rise, an elastic roller follows on the outside, pressit“ the automatically pasted paper firmly to the wall. When the wall papa. hat retched the top, the workman pulls a 3ord, and the paper is neatly clipped off at the level at which it stops.

Soma German arclueologists have made a disc.-wary of the highest interest at Priene. in Asia Miner. A whole city Is being unearthed, in almost as good preservation as Pompeii. Up to the present no similar discovery has been made that gives precise indications of the general arrangement of a Greek clty, of its public monuments, or its individual dwel- lings. Tim city thus exhumed is assuredly of the period of greatest Greek beauty ; the streets cross at right angles and are laid out with the greatest regularity, and we can identify colonnades, theatres, market places, shops, and houses with their decorations and interior arrangements. WHAT TO SEE AT "THE GREATEST

SHOW ON EARTH."

On Saturday next, Messrs. Barnum and Bailey will visit Warrington with their "greatest show on earth." The show will b 3 established oa one of the large fields d Wilderspool Cause way, and immediately be- ahiajoindthineg newly erected "Boathouse Inn." By the courtesy of Messrs. Barnum and Bailey's manager, we have been able to provide our readers with the following account of what to see at this wonderful, and world- famed exhibition of all that can interest, amuse, and in- struct the multitude. THE MENLGERIE.

It is customary to enter the show as soon as the doors are open, and spend an hour before the performances begin in the menagerie teat. And a crowded hour it is, for everything is full of interest, and much is novel. A great proportion of the time laity be spent in looking at the wild animals, which are kept iu 30 or 40 cages. Let no one stay away from any feeling that, as this is a Itravelling menagerie, it must necessarily be an un- pleasant evil-smelling Plass- It is not so. Among all the various animals' there is not one whose calmer would repel spectators ; in fact, the way in which everything is 'kept clean is one of the surprises of the show. Arranged lin a semi-circle at one end of the tent, which is elliptical in shape, are the elephante, ranging in size from huge bulls ten feet high to " babies " ten years old, willing to take any amount of food from the spectators, in addition to their regular supply of hay, which runs to about two tons a day. Leaving the elephants for a walk round the cages, one comes to the seals and see lions, and soon latter them to the hippopotamus and rhinoceri:ie, two huge ungainly creatuaes, the first with a mouth about as !big as an oven, and the second with a hide that success- fully resists bullets. There are two cages of lions, one containing two handsome males, and the other a female, and her three cubs, which were barn in London in IJanuary last. The tigers, particularly the two males, are among the finest animals in the show, their great proportions and beautiful markings making them objects of strength and beauty. The leopards are in an adjoining wagon and are fine specimens ot their species. The giant tapir of Malay demands more than a cursory glance. Those who have visited some of the better menageries may be familier with the South American tapir, but he is only about helf as big as his Malayan brother, an animal of great length, made censpicioua by his short legs and overhanging snouttliks a trunk in miniature. There are not many tapirs like this in captivity. The zoo, as our guide informed us, nee never sucseeded in keeping one more than six months, but Barnuin's have had theirs more than three years. The poor thing caught cold at Olympia, but he looks healthy enough for all that. There are several Russian wolves, meek, innecont looking things, who have somehow obtained a bad reputation among the Russians. The hyenas are amusing beasts, they laugh a good deal. The behaviour ef the pumas be- hind the bars give one the impression that if met in their American hunting grounds they would prove some- what intractable. Their roaring concerts, which happen pretty frequently, are quite as satisfactory a demonstra- tion of their tempers as ordinary visitors to tee show desire. A much pleasanter sound is the chattering of the monkeys There is a cage full of them, and their antics never fail to amuse. But we cannot mention all the animals in all the cages. Thera are polar bans and brown bears, a fine selection of antelepes, kangaroos, deer, gnus, and many others. Down the centre of the tent stand the camels, the drnme laries, and zebras, and near them are the llamas from the Andes, the yak from Central Asia, the water buffalo from South Africa, the Burrneee sacred cattle, the ox with three horns, and the horse with the rubber skin and no hair. Ira a cage to themselves are Johanna and the mandrill. Johanna is the intellectual gorilla, the queen of the menagerie. She eats, drinks, and smokes like a man, combs her hair, cleans her teeth, washes her cage, and use% ponder brush and other dainty articles of the toilet just like any human belog—in fact, she does almost everything but talk. The brilliantly coloured mandrill, with a face whose prevailing colours are bright blue and red, does none of these things. In intellectual development he is far inferior to Johanna ; he stands at the opposite simian pole. THE FREAKS.

The human freaks are also exhibited in this tent, occupying a platform in the oentre. A. very pleasant, smiling family of healthy-looking oddities they are,and as the showman introduces them in turn to the audi- ence, they look as good-humourel as the most pleased spectat6r. There is nothing revolting abont them, all fears of " creepiness" are removed at sight of them. ye.in, whose face, like his father's, is covered with a thick growth of long silky hair,and whe,whea his body is hidden behind a blanket, closely resembles a skye terrier, is an interesting personage. lie chats with the other freaks, speaking English as well as his native Russian. Hermann, the Hungarian youth, can ex- pand his chest lin inches, and break a steel chain by the force of the expansion. James Morris has a skin of wonderful elasticity, which he can draw away from his I arms and cheeks and chest to a great exter‘t ; he can almost burr his head in the skin of his ch-st. Twol hairy wonders are Miss Annie Jones, the bearded lady, and Mille. Ivy, whose hair clusters round her head like moss. Lallo3 and Lalla are one person, and Leila is the I name of Lalloala second body. Lalloo is a young man of 23, and his smoadb!dy is that of a female which has no . separate head,but joined to him just below the sterinn. Lalloo'a case is undoubtedly one of" arreste I twins," the union of the two bodies being carried t 3 a much, I greater degree than in the case of the Siamese twins. A great contrast is that between Queen Mats, a smiling little woman standing 22i inches hip, and Leah May, a I winsome giantess woose height is about Vt. 4in. Charles Tripp, the armless wonder, site near the numb tatted HowardS, the skeleton dude, who stands 51t 9in. high and weighs 4 stones 4lbs., is close by, and not far away is Dalva Fritz. who regularly swallows swords, and now and then putS down his throat a 22inch bayonet, and on top .of that a musket, waich he fires off. There is Saida Galla, the Egyptian enchantress, to see, and as one strolls around he hears the howling of the dervishes, as they pe.rforna . their svhirling dances, and the sweet music which Savo produces frain his glasses. THE CIRCUS PERFORMANCE. And so, after a full hour has been very enjayably spent in the contemplation of these wonders brought I from all parts of the earth, the spectator turns into the I circus teat, where a two houra' performance is soars in lull swing. As an instance of the ease taken for the Ibenefit of the public in every part of the show, we may mention that ths weight-carrying capacity of the whole of the :seating is strongly tested before a performance is given in any new town. The programme informs us that there are to be 15 dis- plays, followed by the hippodrome and chariot races with 14 events. Display No. lis a processional entry of some of the anim‘ls and their riders, the steeds—borses, camels, and elephants—all gaily caparisoned, and the performArs bswigged and bedecked in gorgelus fashion. The hinder phrtion of the procession is supposed to rep- resent the Roy al ovation tendered by Ferdinand and Isabella to Columbus on his return from the discovery of the Western world ; and this is why we have 15th cen tiny costumes and trappings for the horses. The pro- cession has no sooner disappeared than the elvphants are again seen advancing along the track. Wait are celled baby elephants file into the centre ring, and there exhibit their tricks and evolutions ; and we learn that they are only" babies "by comparison, the youngest being at least ten years old. The grown-ups in the other rings are 30 years of age or older. The babies before us are 'cute enough ; they trot round the ring like so many circus horses:they stand on their hind legs; me of them usurps the functions of the ring-master,

and squatting on his haunches,rings a bell with his pro- boscis, and thereby directs the performance of his col- leagues. But an occasional glance at what is going on in the other rings show that the babies have not yet completed their education, for they fail in some of their attempts. They obtain ready grace, however, from the delighted spectators, and leave the ring amid cordial applause. Everything goes like clockwork. The only evi- dence of directon is the periodical ringing of the big bell at whose sound every display promptly ends, sometimes before a particular trick or feat is completed. Follow- ing the elephants come some tumblers, but these are triflers compared with the extraordinary acrobats who appear later on. They yield place to the first equestrian exhibition. In this we have "the new woman supreme In the arena," ring-master,clowne,and riders all being of weaker sex ; and the clowning we have to admit is a little "off," for the ladies do not shine at the noisier I rough-and-tumble humour. But there is no doubt about their cleverness on horeeback. Miss Rose Wentworth is the principal, and she performs a somersault on the bareback horse, a feet which no other eques- trienne can equal. The ethers however, have many daring tricks, and the ease and con- fidence of :their every moment strike one at the first glance. After them comes a " pot-pound "of startling expert performances; and the programme tells us there are ten different sets of performers at work, but one's recollections limit themselves to Orford, the juggler, and a dapper little Jap, gracefully poising on his hands. Display No. Bis a children's number, coa- -1 Meting of feats by troupes of cleverly trained animals— educated pigs, trained seals, dancing dogs and monkeys, and oddities by goats and a baby elephant. One of the dogs executes amid roars of laughter, a very good imitation of the music-hall skirt dance to music, while another pair dons the gloves, and has a harmless little set-to in boxing fashion. In the next display there is another maze of wonders, but it is Monsieur La Rnche,the ascension sphere artiste, who attracts most attention. He hag a bright metal globe, 28 inches in diameter, opening into halves. This just contains him, and when fastened inside he com- mences the ascent of a spiral incline, reaching about 20 feet high. The ball gradually rolls up the incline, rests a moment at the summit, while the performer fires a pistol through a hole in the sphere, and pokes out a bit of bunting, and then it as gradually descends, looking every moment as if it would roll sideways off the track and go crashing down to the platform below. Hew the man moves himself and the sphere remains a mystery even when explained. Inside the sphere is as smooth and as highly polished as outside, and there is no pro- jection to give the least hotel. We are told that the man makes ths ball roll round ;dim. and that he himself never changes his crouching paeition ; but this only serves to make his feat the more marvellous. Meanwhile, among a dozen other performers, a Japanese is remarkable for the deftness with which he ascends a rope at an angle of 45 degrees,balancing himself with a parasol and a fan His ascent completed,he slides down again like a streak of lightning; and one wonders whether the skin is off his toes. A crowd of performers, mostly acrobats and athletes, fill ths other platforms and rings, and from the cadre seats one hears every now and then a beret of applause from the people who have ths end-rings to I themselves.

Display No. 10 draws all eyes, however, to the centre ring, which alone is occupied. The crescendo of as- tonishment which preceding displays afforded, reaches a climax as seventy beautiful horses enter the ring and range theraselves on tiers into an equine pyramid which the shout of the ringmaster sets into swift and ever swifter motion. A prettier sight the show does not pro- vide; and the perfect obedience and the evident sagacity of this mass of beautiful animals are qualities which one's vocabulary fails of words t 3 praise with adequacy. From equestrianism we retain to the acrobats, whose achievements are on an ascending scale of daring and cleverness • then more bare-back riding, in which egPeni ally Miss A.ppolonia Orford, a young lady of only 16, ex- hibits powers whose complete development, we are told, will some day carry her to the very summit of her profession. With similar alternations of riding and athletics we reach Display No. • 14. A double tandem exploit by Miss Nellie Reid comes next prevents us, however, from being dnoccupied, andwhen she and her companions have gone we prepare for the concluding display. To the left the "Five Unparalled Silbons. "in the centre the "Four Unapproachable Dun- hame,"to the left the 'Electrifying Hanlons," exhibit an once the art of acrobatics in its highest development, and the fertility and resource of the compiler of the programme in describing it. The Silbons especially as- tonish us. One of them springs in mid-air across a space of 17ft after he has left his hold of the trapeze, and, contrary to the usual experience, he is not met by his companion with a corresponding spring ; he clears , the whole space himself, and drops a dead weight into the arms of his colleague, who hangs motionless to I await him. The leader winds up the performance with a triple somersault, and most dangerous of all acrobatic feats. A few seasons ago two performees in close succession broke their neck; in attempting it. Anything short of the full three turns would be fatal, we are told for the performer must drop on his back into the net, and only on his back, if he wants to rise again alive. We have little space left for the hippodrome races, which are exciting like all horse races,and more exciting , than most. They are real races, too, for the riders get a small premium if they run home first ; and there is he advantage obtainable by them from knowing their movements. The fact is, they don't know them ; they draw lota before each race as to which horse they shall ride. The force and fury of the running is only limited by the nature of the track. A coursing contest between three English whippet% is an especial favourite, where sporting instincts are so well developed ; and the chil- dren and their elders, too, laugh heartily at the spec- tacle of a pony race with menkeys up. The hurdle rac- ing and jumping are better than anything at an agricul- tural show, and the chariot racing, which is quite equal to the lithographs on the walls and in the shop windows, fittingly puts the crown to a lick-creation performance.

- - Messn. Barnum and Bailey's great street pageant and procession will take plac3 between 9 and 10 o'cl,-;ek ett Saturday morning. The big street free parade will b 3 a wonderful and stupendous speztacle of men, women, children, hews, elephants, wild beasts, chariots, cars and floats. Christopher Columbus, as well as scores of other characters famous in his time, are all truthfully represented, costumed in the same rich manner then in vogue in royal robes of state, on horseback surrounded with their escorts, in costly uniforms, singly.in groups, in picturesque and cherming style, and all historically correct,just as they appeared in Barcelona in 1492,when they returned from their first voyage of discovery. Lead- ing the pageant is ens of the most magnificient Sights imaginable, that of a wondsrous team of 40 fine bay horses driven by 030 man. Preceding the Columbus portion of the procession are rare displays of open dens of wi,d beasts and carved golden chariots,containing the splendid zoological collection, followed by 20 e/ephants, and allegorical chariots, illustrating nursery rhymes and children's fairy stories. The whole affair is grand and inspiring.

NATIONAL AmdLGAMA.TBD SOCIETY OF OPERATIVE HOUSE AND SIIIP PAINTERS AND DECORATORS.—

WARRINGTON BRANCH. The above branch held their :first annual dinner at the Old House at Home,Friars'-green,when about 40 members were present,the catering of Mr. and Mrs. Mullin giving every satisfaction. Brother Grounds (president of the branch) presided,and the following toasts were submitted and responded to : "The Queen," "The N.A.S. of House and Ship Pain- ters and Decorators (Warrington Branch) ;" "The rest of the Building Trades." Brother G. M. Sur,ley J.P., general secretary, from Manchester,gave an in- teresting account of the progress of the society from its formation. A presentation was made to Brother Howard of a beautifully framed emblem subscribed for by the members, for his service as secretary. Brother F. B. Mellor commenced the musical portion of the evening with a selection on the piano, followed by songs rendered in a first-class manner by Brothers E. Unsworth, Gleeson, Lelland' Jennings, Glaze, Jackson, Mr. Mullin, and Mr. Pot- ter. Votes of thanks were accorded to the Chair- man for presiding, and to the host and hostess. An enjoyable evening was brought to a close by the company singing "Auld Lang Syne.'

WARRINGTON.

THE JUBILEE OF MUSEUM AND ART GALLERY. Arrangements are now in progress for holding an art exhibition in the Gymnasium and Parr Hall during October in celebration of the Jubilee year of the Museum and Art Gallery at Warrington. The Museum, Library and Arts Committee have ap- pointed an Art Exhibition Committee, of which Councillor Alfred Woods is chairmen, to negotiate for the loan of pictures and make the necessary Ilpreparations. The Warrington Museum and Art Gallery was opened in 1848 and the committee de- sire to celebrate its 50th anniversary by getting to- gether a collection of really first-class works of art. The committee have already received promises of support from some of the most celebrated artists, land also from the owners of important pictures. All the hanging arrangements have been placed in the hands of Messrs. Thomas Agnew and Sons Man- chester, and there seems every probability that this Jubilee celebration of art in Warrington will be a I success. _

It is intended to hold promenade concerts daily in the Parr Hall which will be hyphened with the IGymnasium—where the exhibition of pictures will take place—by a covered way 90 feet long and 10 feet wide. Both buildings will be lighted by electricity supplied by the Warrington and district Electric Light and Power Company, Limited, of which Mr. C. L. Clarke is manager. The Probyn Ladies' Orchestra has been engaged for the promen- ade concerts during the opening week, and will give two performances daily. The contributions of pictures to the exhibition have been most encouraging and as an indication of their value it may be stated that the estimated Iworth of those already offered has been put at £lOO,OOO. Mr. VV 11. Lever, of Thornton Manor, ICheshire, has promised to lend several of the best examples of the late Sir John Millais' work,and the committee have also promises of work by the late Lord Leighton. The Manchester Corporation have magnanimously promised the loan of ." The Venetians "by Luke IFildes, R.A.. and "One Hundred Years Ago" by George Sheffield, This litter picture is considered to be certainly one of the best of Mr. Sheffield's pro- ductions.

Mr. Holbrook Gaskell, of Woelton Wood, near Liverpool, will contribute a magnificent collection of black and white drawings which have never been exhibited before, of nearly thirty pictures by Luke Fildes, called the Lord Brackenbury collec- tion. In addition Mr. Gaskell's collection will include two of the finest examples of the work of David Cox, as well as" The Return of the Peni- tent" by Luke Fildes; the first Academy picture of Mrs. Fildes "On the Grand Canal " ; one of Mr. 'Alma Tadema's productions; Mr. Henry Woods, R.A.'s "014 Master " ; Mr. Van Haanen'a "The Cobbler's Shop, Venice" ; Mrs. E. M. Ward's " Challerton," as well as two celebrated pictures by Turner; Birket Foster's " Teaching Dolly to Dance," and two fine examples of the work of Wm Hunt.

The Corporations of Oldham and Liverpool have promised a loan of pictures from their Art Galleries and these will be selected by the committee, Colonel William H. Walker is sending to the ex- hibition fine samples of the work of J. D. Watson, Basil Bradley and Adrian Jones. Mr. W. H. Bleckley will send Lierkomer's pictures "By the mar,zin of the willow-veiled" "Stackpole' and "In the Doldrums" together with others. Councillor John Crosileld, J.P., who is acting with Couneillor Wools as collector of the pictures has promised to lend his picture " The Spanish war of Independency" by F. Goodall. B,A.,"The Forum;" by Ansdell, R.A. and a picture by Copley Fielding. -Alderman Henry Roberts will lend "The Monk'" by Dendy Sadler and his Mrs. Allingham picture. Mr. Robert Garnett, J.P. is sending samples of the work of Mr. Clarence Whaite.

Mr. W. A. Toplis, promises his" Fairy Grotto " ' and" Nature's Architecture," both of which have appeared in the Royal Academy. , Mr. R. H. Edmondson will contribute the well- known picture by George Sheffield "The Fishing Boat in the North sea " and " The Lancashire Pit Brow Lass" by Arthur Wasse. Mrs. Beamont has offered a collection of pictures including one by David Cox and another by Van- dycke. Mr. William Long, J.P., will contribute a very fine collection consisting of E. M. Ward's "Fire of London," and" Hurley Lock on the Thames" to- gether with works by Copley Fielding and Whimple. Messrs. Agnew and Sons of Manchester will show a collection of the finest work of Edwin Long R.A., George Leslie, R.A., Val Prinsep, A.R.A„ Robert Alacbeth, A.R.A. In addition Mrs. Thomas Agnew will lend the exhibition the Rae picture in her possession by W. P. Frith, R.A., entitled "Ilogarth arrested as a Spy and brought before the Tribunal at Calais."

Promises of other picture are expeeted,but from the list already given, it will be seen that the exhi- bition will include a fine collection of art.

THE REVOLT IN CRErE.

===== 0002601_18980930_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
INTERESTING LETTER_FROM A BRITISH

OFFICER.

Mr. K. 3/19iibbin, industrial trainer at the Pad- gate Industrial Schools, has received the following interesting letter from his brother,First Class Petty Officer T. 31`Kibbin, new serving on board H.M.S. Camperdown off the Island of Crete. (coPY) : H.M.S. Camoerdown, Crete, Mediterranean, Sep- tember llth,lB9B.—Dear Bob, I think you are sure to see about this turn out in Crete and I want you to send me the accounts in the English papers. I atn sure, as everyone of the fleet here is, English, French, Russian and Italian, that the people of Europe have no idea, cannot in fact believe the sights that take place here with Mussulmans and Christians. Our men have been shot down like flies ; about a 100 Turks to one of us. We had the officer and 11 men killed belonging to the Highland Light Infantry, and H M. S. Hazzard and cad to take them to sea to bury them as we could not bury them ashore. We have on board our ship at present 7 wounded with stabs and bullet wounds one of them in four places. The refugees who come off are in a shocking state, principally the women and children who are st,ibbed and shot in all man- ner of places. One poor young woman had her breast ripped open in 3 places and had to fly to the boats all but naked. We have on board 300 re- fugees, the other foreign men-of-war members in proportion. As this part of the Island is under our protection the most cases come off to us. There were over 700 Christians killed on the 6th and 7th inst., babies cut in two; heads chopped off, won en outraged and their houses looted and burned to the ground. When we arrived there on Tuesday night, the town (Candia) seemed ablaze and so it was in the part, where the Christian population lived. The town of Candi& has a wall right round it, and our troops are somehow hemmed in. There are only about 500 of them here and they estimate the Turks at about 40,000 to 50,000 strong. We have not found out whether the Turkish Troops are in league with the Civil population, but if it turns out to be so, we are going to bombard the place as soon as the reinforcements arrive from Malta. Well Bob. I hope you are all quite well at home, and also by the time you receive this letter that the place will be blown down over their heads, as we are getting tired of waiting. Our guns are all ready. Our big 67 ton guns at one minute's notice.--From your loving brother TOM. P.S.—I hope this is not the last.

APPOINTMENT OF THE ginv WARRINGTON PCST-

MASTER.

Mr. J. L. Viney, postmaster of Douglas, has been appo.nted postmaster of Warrington,in place of Mr. West, who has gone to Sunderland. It is not yet known when Mr. Viney will enter upon his duties at Warrington, but it may be interesting to note that he has previously held the following appoint- ments :-1880, Postmaster of Sitingbourne ; 1892, Ramsgate ; 1895, Douglas.
