BURNING A WARRINGTON 2IUN'B

EFFIGY.

SEQUEL TO • POLICE CASE. RIOTOUS SCENES.

At the Borough Police Court on Monday before thr Mayor (in the chair) and other magistrates Annie Dew' r son, 11, Jolley-street, and Margaret McCabe, Maria Jenks and Julia Jenks, of West-street, were summoned for committing a breach of the peace by being disorderly in Kerfoot-street on the 16th inst.—Defendants pleading not guilty. The Chief Constable said this ease a 1036 out of an tut- fortunate cage which was heard at the court on Friday week. A man named Atherton appeared on a charge of deserting his wife, and in consequence of certain allege. tines made against the wife the eerie was dismissed. Al a sort of comment upon the decision of the magistrates a large crowd of people, amongst whom were the four defendants, went to the man's house the same night, burned his effigy in front of the door, broke windows with stones, and altogether behaved like savages and barbarians. That conduct went on for about an hour. Sergeant Smith said he was on duty in Winwiek- road on the night of Friday, the 16th September,and he beard of a disturbance which was proceeding in Ken oat- street. lie went there and received certain information, upon which he made a report. The street in front of Atherton's house was covered with sods, and two panes of glass were broken in a back window. Later the same night he went to the houses of the defendants. Mrs. Da191012 Atherton's daughter, admitted that she was there and threw one sod. Mrs. McCabe and the younger Jenks also admitted being there, and Mrs. Jenks denied that she was. A witness named Houlding,a master file eutter,carrying on business in Kerfootatreet, said he was reading in the house about half-past nine when he heard a terrific noise in the street. He ran to the front door, and saw a crowd of what seemed to be four or five hundred people round Atherton's house. They seemed to have collected from all the slums in Warrington. They burnt some sort of an effigy and someone kicked it against the deer. Witness sent for the police,and Sergeant Smith Came along. The disturbance seemed to have been a set plan. The crowd of people at the top of the street watched the °Meer pass on his beat, and they then knew they would have an hour. When the Sergeant came the people for the most part had dispersed. Tale was not the first time such a thing had taken place, and he thought the law- abiding portion of the people in the neighbourhood should he protected. Thomas Atherton, 24, Kerfootestreet, said a certain charge against him was dismissed by the magistrates on Friday week. The same night a number of people came to his home. He was standing outside 'smoking his pipe when the crowd came down the street. They commenced to make a fire. Be saw his daughter, Mrs. Dawson,there. His daughter deliberately took a brand from the fire and threw it towards the window. She and several of the crowd then commenced to throw sods. When the fire was made they commenced to throw stones and a window was broken at the back. The disturbance lasted about an hour. Thomas Atherton, jun., son of the last witness, said It large crowd of people came to his father's house on the Friday night. They shouted and hooted and burned an effigy. Witness saw the four defendants. They threw stones and sods. Mrs. McCabe said she would pull wit- ness out of the house and hit him. John James Rutter, 34, Kerfoot-strest, said he was talking to Atherton at twenty minutes past nine, when the crowd collected. Mrs. Dawson set fire to the effigy. The crowd shouted and made all sorts of noises. There were about 50 or 60 people there and they threw some sods. He saw Mrs. Jenks there, but she took no part fa the disturbance. Mrs. Dawson threw a stone and broke a window. Margaret Atherton gave corroborative evidence. She said, however, that she did not see Mrs. Jenks there. Defendant Dawson said that when she went down to the house there was a fire. She told her father to turn that woman out and not to disgrace himself. Defendant here burst into tears and had to be assisted to a seat. Mr. McCabe and the younger Jenks denied that they took part in the disturbance, while Mrs. Jenks said she was not there. A Mrs. Wilkinson said Mrs. Jenks was at her own house in West-street all the evening. The case aeainst her and the younger Jenks was dismissed, and the other defendants were bound over in the sum of £5 each to keep the peace for six months, and ordered to pay the costs.—The Mayor remarked that he sympatisel with the children of Antherton to a certain extent,as the ease before referred to was the most disgraceful that hid ever come before the Court.

HE LIMBER 30th, 1898.

THE annual report of the Educational Committee of Council on Edu- cation whick has been issued this week, is more than a month later than usual. The delay may be due to the fact that the recent legislation establishing Associations lof Voluntary Schools, and increasing the " aid " grants, has thrown more work on the DepArtrnent. A month later though it is, the elementary school statistics are only brought down to August 31s1,1897,which further illustrates the slow movements of the official mind when it has t) do with the compila- tion and tabulation of facts and figurei. The re- port, generally speaking, is fairly satisfactory as a record of progress. But even Liberal educa- tionists will be ready Lo acknowledge that the Dims of DEVONSIIIRE and Sir JoHN Gone; who are responsible for it, have not spared the defects of the present system. The increase in the number of children receiving ele- mentary education has not been so great during the past year as in the previous year, but a re- duction of the rate of increase must be expected as the whole population comes under the opera- tion of the Education Acts. Expre3sed in per- centages, the number of scholars on the register increased in 1897 1.54 per cent., as compared with 2.33 per cent, in the previous year. The increase in average attendance %Minot quite so good-1•48 per cent., as compared with 2.26. The report complains much of "leakages." Compulsion in the country districts appears to be la z and ineffective, especially in the case of children over twelve. Among the agricultural population the old idea still prevails that " elementary education pro- duces a disinclination among many of the more promisini children to enter upon a life of agricul- tural labour." It is pointed out also that "rural educational authorities, who are the sole authori- ties for enforcing school attendance, and for pre- venting the employment of children before they have fulfilled certain educational requirements, are sometimes themselves among those who are anxious to obtain cheap child labour at certain times of the year." They are, therefore, "apt to refuse to fix a reasonably high standard " in their by-laws, and sometimes refrain altogether from enforcing those; by-laws. Thus self-interest still operates to prevent our educational system from becoming truly national. It is to be feared, how- ever, that it is not only in agricultural districts this is the case. The Voluntary Schools, and the efforts of their promoters, still tend, more or less, to divert to sectarianism what was meant for education, both in the form of funds and administrative energy. Touching the

vi t

employment of children of school ago, the Depart- I ment is preparing a special return, which should prove useful to educational reformers who desire to raise the age of exemption from manual labour for young children. At the same time it must be . admitted that this question is one attended with considerable difficulty. The report points out that the employment of school children in such light work as that of caddies on golf links may benefit • Ithem by keeping them in the open air. Who half-timers are abolished, and the age of employ- ment raised, it will, perhaps be unwise to draw line,too tightly around "casual employmf It is, on the whole, satisfactory to find that, pite certain drawbacks which some of our t tinental nations have not to contend against, are making progress educationally, and our Schi Boards are gradually raising the national of enlightenment. - in It

WHEN things do move The Chinese China they move rapidly. Transformation. _ t mIY

hat

is only a few days ago tJ we heard of the palace revolt, and now, not oi is the young EPtIPEROR deposed and almost f( gotten, and the EMPRESS-DOWAGER reigning in stead, but all his reform decrees have been al gated, and his principal advisers are in prisc among them being CHANG YIN ROAN, Li He Caeleu's great rival. Li HUNG CHANG himself,wl was but the other day dismissed from the penal service, is "topside " again. He is tically all-powerful now. It is even said that lEntea ma wants to marry him, but that may be icanard. Apparently the late EMPEROR was going I too fast, with his new Western ideas of reform. He was a young man in a hurry. The EMPRESS' DOWAGER warned him that he was let. ting his zeal run away with his discretion but when it came to a decree against the sacred pigtail, the EMPRESS, being a strong minded lady, could stand it no longer, and immediately asserted the superiority o f her sex, turned her hopeful nephew out of the palace, and put in prison as many of his friends as she could lay hands on. Now the old order is restored in China, and the Mandarins are no longer in fear of their purses or their pigtails. It is something to China's credit that this revolution has been carried out bloodlessly. But, perhaps, that is only another proof of the phlegmatic dispo- sition of the Celestials. How stand British inter - ests, the open door, and the railway concessior4 under the new rig fine? As yet we are in the dark on this question. Some of the reports from China have been ominous and disquieting. The British fleet has been moved about with consider- able celerity ; but apparently the net result h. ' been that a few war ships steamed from Wei-lue'. Wei up to Taku and then steamed back again, without accomplishing more than was achieved by the DUKE of YORK and his twenty thousand men on a memorable occasion. The Russian influenc has had something to do with tho coup d'etat is' more than probable. Li Hubro's family are said, tobe under the protection of the Russians Oe PORT ARTHUR, and Li Iliallo himself, who is back in the Imperial confidence, iS known to be patronised, if not paid,. by Russia. Then it is stated from Pekin that lisu-Yineo-Yi, who was removed from the Tsung-li-Yamen at the instance of Sir NICHOLAS O'CONOR, for breach of faith, has been rc appointed a member of diat body. All news in- dicates that British influence is no longer in the ascendant. Although M. PAN-Lore has gene Sir CLAUDE MACDONALD has :not succeeded him as dictator-in-chief to the Court of Pekin. The re- flection must occur to many, who have intelli- gently followed events in both the Far and th near East, that, had Sir CLiUDE MACDONALD Only acted with the decision and firmness that Admire? Noy. had recently shewn in Crete, Great Britain' influence would be stronger than it is in China to- day. Perhaps, however, any weakness in this direction must be charged to the Foreign Office home, not to our Minister at Pekin. It is certain that the wrangles between the English Arabes' seder and the Russian • Charge d AffaireB have tended to lower the prestige of both Powers in the eyes of the Celestials. Our differences with Russia ought to have been settled in London or St. Petersburg. We could then have spoken in tones more likely to be heeded at Pekin. Just iiner the Powers might do worse than take the advice given by a German paper, and "instead Of wrangling about concessions decide upon conarnen action "in the interests of all civilised Powers. Coming from the quarter it does, this advice is not' disinterested, but it is to the purpose.

At a meeting of Congregationalists of the Liver- pool district, of which Warrington forms a part, e resolution was carried protesting in strong rerrae against the sacerdotal doctrines and practices ilr troduced into the Church of England, which theY consider a national danger and call for the iol- mediate interference of Parliament. Protests of 8 similar nature have been frequent of late, not onl', from without the Church "as by law Established/ but from within. There was a remarkable scene at the Church Congress itself on Wednesday wheo Mr. Kensit who has been conspicuous in his agitv tion against Ritualism met with a very war' not to say boisterous—reception. The usuallY suave gentlemen who constituted his heare.re shouted at him ; they called him a liar, and twlce the Bishop had to ask them to show fair plaY. This pretty quarrel amongst members of tlr National Church is not edifying but it 15 very interesting to outsiders and serves to show the vast amount of perturbation which the Romish practices have caused. It has been arguedl,. by some of the leaders of the Church that Noir conformists have no voice in what is going 05 within the Established Church, and that it is for , Churchmen to decide what doctrines shall ,be taught, or what Ritualistic practices shall be troduced. This is rather a narrow-minded yieW te take of such an important question. When , eci , church which claims to be national teaches 0„0 practices doctrines which are likely to undo t" work of the Reformation, Nonconformists have 55 right as citizens and as Evangelical Protestaut„ to interfere. And that they are determined f)'' do so is exemplified by the resolution passed at t : meeting of Congregationalists at Liverpool 0' Tuesday --- _

Of the many suggestions made last year for celebrating the Diamond Jubilee of Her Majesty ..,the Queen in Warrington a prominent place was %wen to that of an Art Exhibition. The proposal *lst with general acceptance, but after being talked about, and then postponed, it was even- tually.„ struck off the programme altogether. When nis decision was arrived at it was thought that the last had been beard for some time to come of !In art exhibition in Warrington. But it is now being revived again in a somewhat modified and raore practical form. This time it is to celebrate .4 jubilee of an entirely different kind. The 4fluseum and Art Gallery it appears were duly °Paned in 1848, and the committee are desirous of celebrating the institution's 50th anniversary by getting together a collection of really first- class works of art. And what is more tO the point there is every probability of the corn- taittee succeeding in doing so. Last year, it will be remembered there was a noticeable apathy about an art exhibition in quarters where there should have been most enthusiasm. But all this is now changed. The Art Exhibition Corn- ittee is busily engaged in the work of making the necessary arrangements, and already pictures to the value of £lOO,OOO 'nave been promised for the exhibition. Such a collection as that which is Promised for inspection next month has never been acen in the town during its long history, and it Should prove an unique and commendable cele- bration of art in a town which has had within its borders many men who have made their mark in '4e world of art.

On Monday evening John Littlemare, boatman, in the employment of the Auderton Company, was driving bis horse near Preston Brook tunnel on the Bridgewater Canal, when the animal suddenly attacked him and knocked him down. The horse then trampled upon him. His hand was so mutilated that three fingers bad to be amputated, and he was otherwise seriously injured. ACCIDENT ON THE SNIP CANAL AT RUNCORN. On Monday an accident betel a steamer bound for Manchester a t the Swing Bridge, Runcarn. Owing to some defect in the machinery the bridge would not open properly, and the steamer collided with it while at an angle, and was driven aground, after sustaining con- siderable damage to her top sail, kc. She was eventu- ally got off by some tugs, and proceeded on her journey.

'RUNCORN NOTES. --- [By A CORRESPONDEN]]

The decision of the Board of Guardians in refus- ing an applicant—because she kept a publichouse —who applied for sanction to adopt a young girl, an inmate of 14 years of age, was dictated by pru- dence. There could be no personal objection to the applicant, who was apparently influenced by the most commendable and kindly feeling, and who would probably have taken every care of her charge. But all common sense people, whether teetotallers are not, will realize at once the great danger there would be in allowing an impression- able girl to be installed in a public-house, even as the adopted daughttor of the landlady. Temptation would beset her at every turn, with possible conse- quences to a moral character which has not had the strict training of respectable home life, that are not difficult to foresee. The Guardians acted wisely in not making a precedent which might be followed by serious consequences. OF course it is purely a matter of principle, and rather hard on the well- meaning applicant, but the Guardians are right in setting that principle on a high pedestal. They hold a grave responsibility in their control over the lives of the young inmates of the workhouse.

I notice that a rumour as to there being serious difficulties in the way of the electric lighting of Runcorn is being circulated, but how much there is in it remains to be seen. I have beard such reports so often, that personally I put little credence in them.

FOOTBA LL. The result of the match between Runcorn and Oldham, is a matter to deplore, at it is by no means representative of the game. Of course, Old- ham were the best fifteen, and a" good bit" the best, yet still that " bit "would never be represen- ted by 24 points, Then how did it come about?

--- It was due in some measure to the slowness of the referee, that the "bottle-greens" were "bottled- "to the tune of 27 points to 3. The conte3t wag a good one, being fast and open; and had Oldham been satisfied with playing the strict game, there would have been nothing to mar an other-wise capi- tal display of the Northern Union code.

As it happened Oldham were allowedlto indulge in some exceedingly sharp, and reprehensible prac- tices. They "knocked-on," "passed forward," slipped in "offside," and last bilt not least impeded their opponents when on the point of tackling the catcher or scorer. Still they played a superior game and would have won independently of these blem- ishes. But if they had not indulged in them they would hardly have beaten Runcorn by such a large margin.

As an illustration that "The champions" did not have all their own way, it is interesting to note that Charlie Butterworth (probably the youngest three-quarter back in the First Division) scored a try against them. And further, Runcorn only had 14 men out in the second half, Jolley being badly hurt. Thus Runcorn were playing against Oldham, shady practices, and bad luck—quite enough to overwhelm any team.

A. word in praise of the Runcorn forwards! They were the best of the two packs, and on Saturday's form will prove a thorn in the flesh of a good many teams before the close of the competition. They dribbled superbly,tand some of their runs made the Oldham crowd quite envious. S. Walker, J.Hughes, and Gayter,to say nothing of Lightfoot,got through some excellent work.

The Runcorn A team were also beaten on Satur- day, and Oldham A romped home with 14 points to nil. The game was a spirited, but one sided one. This result is little to be wondered at, when it is considered that the Oldham "A "is practically com- posed of reserves—(and some of them " stars" at that), to the Oldham first; and that the Oldham club could put two teams on the fiald very nearly equal to each other.

On Saturday Leigh, who are in about the same boat as Runcorn,pay a visit here. What makes the game of a little interest—is the doubt as to which team is the worst, as both fifteens got beaten heavily on Saturday.—Leigh at Broughton by 29 points to 2 points, and Runcorn at Oldham by 27 points to 3 points. The Runcorn team has been selected but there is still some doubt as to its final composition.

Runcorn Athletic Association team, are a number of young enthusiasts, who are trying hard to estab- lish some kind of an association team in Runcorn. But so far their attempts have resulted in big de- feats. They are a persevering lot, however, and mean to make a stir. On Saturday they play Wid- nes Vies., at home (Canal-street ground). The team is selected as follows :—Goal, Stanway ; backs, Worthington, and McLeod ; halves, Gleave.Burrows, and Bell; forwards, Lunt, Hilton, Hulse, Hopwood, and Williams, with Caruthers, Dumbell, and Law- ton as reserves.

THE LEST OF FOLLY BaooK SYPHON._

Mr. W. Henry Hunter engineer to tne Manchester Ship Canal Company wrote as follows with reference to the Fully Brook Syphon :—I have yours of yesterday's date with copy of communication from the surveyor enclosed therein. ks a matter of fact ever since the alterations referred to by Mr. Diggle were carried out, masses of sand with which they were not previously troubled have been carried down into the syphon. I think it would be well in order to arrive at some agree- menc as to the facts if your Surveyor were to meet Mr. Congreve the resident engineer of the ground. I will be obliged if you will instruct Mr. Diggle to do this com- municating with him direct as to the time of sueh meet- ing. Mr. Diggle stated that since he had had an interview with Mr. Congreue last Tuesday and they had agreed that there must be some mistake, and that the stud got in evidently from somewhere else, so teat the matter was at an end so far as the Council were concerned.

MOORE SEWERAGE MATTERS. The Committee who bad had under consideration the question of the sewage disposal from houses belonging to Miss Miller and others in the township of Moore went over the ground and visited the existing sewer oa the 23rd inst. They decided "that such sewer,if cleaned out from time to time as may be necessary, is sufficient for present requirements, but if Mr. Gandy would like to fill up the existing ditch-sewer and divert the sewage across the adjoining field the committee see no objection to his being allowed to do SO, provided he makes the neceasary arrangements with the Manchestee Ship Canal Ceinpany and puts in a pipe sewer sufficiently large to take the water and sewage whirh is now pro- vided for by the ditch-sewer, and takes upon himself all responsibility for the diversion. The committee'e report was adopted on the motion of Mr. Darlington, seconded by Mr. Gleave. THE PEOPOSED ISOLATION HOSPITAL. Mr. J. Mosley Williams repert ed on benalf of the Court, who had had under consideration the question of the provision of an Isolation Hospital in the Fro dsham District, and said they found a hospital with 10 beds could be erected for £3,500. Before going any further I in the negotiations they considere I it necessary for them ' to come to some conclusion, as to whether they would be juatified in spending that atneuet of money. For that sum the committee felt that the nospital could be built. They would furnish the details at a subsequent meeting, but in the meantime they would have to decide as to wnether they were prepared to go to that ex- pense. Mr. C. E. Linaker said it appeared to him that the 4 site suggested, although favourable, in some respect WAS open to objection in others. Tne Local Government Board had intimated on a previous occasion that such hospitals should be built about a mile away from any dwelling-house. He believed a site was offered in Frod- sham, which was considered to be, by many people, a suitable one. Dr. Adams told them on Saturday that he did not think that the Local Government Board would sanction that site, as it WAS within about 500 yards of a dwelling-house. If that building would be so objection- able, he theught it would be unfair to plant down an infectious hospital amid some of the best building land IR Frodsham if a road was opened out. Personally he was not so sweet upon the hospital question, especially if it was to cost sore e £3,500. The matter then dropped.

VISIT TO PANTLSAPH.

Mr. T. Wright submitted the report of the ClamMee which visited Pantaiaph Orphanage. They were pleased with their visit. They found the children who were from Runcorn, quite happy and they did not wish to come baek. There was a very nice farm there, and thy had a number of cows which provided them with all their milk. They also grew all tneir vegetables. He found the lady superior was the nicest lady he had ever , seen (laughter.) There were about 40 s;sters who all ' came t 3 assist in the work, which they did for nothing. And some of the young ladies were the prettiest he had ever seen. (laereaved daughter.) On the whole they were pleased with Chair viait, which he assured them would be quite a treat to those who had not been there, when it came to their turn. TELE NEW VACCINATION ACT.

A letter was read from the Local Government Board stating that in consequence of the new Vaccination Act the Use of stations at which public vaccination has hitherto been performed will be discontinue I at the end of this year except in tho3e cases,where under Section 7 of the Act the Board deem it expedient " by reason of serious risk of outbreak of small-pax or of other excep- tional circumstances "to require the Guardians to pro- vide vaccination stations. A table of pauperism in the Counties of Cheshire and Lancashire compiled from official returns was presented by Mr. H. Jenner-Fast. gener..l inspector. There was a considerable increase shown.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

CENTRAL WARD VACANCY. UNOPPOSED RETURN OF MR. REYNOLDS.

Although three candidates entered the field for the honour of representing Central Ward on the Board of Guardians at Dutton, as was expected, one of them secured a seat without opposition. At first it was gener- ally considered that the vicar (the Rev. Canon W ood) would sit, but it is evident that he felt justified in retir- ing, as the duties of Guardian, if diseharged with his usual conscientiousness, would only mean increased tax- ation upon fully taxed time. Mr. W. Handley, who is the other retiring candidate, leaving the field open to Mr. T. Reynolds, Norman-road, does so with his usual consideration for the ratepayers, and thus eaves the ward from the expense of an election:—of Mr. Thomas Reynolds, who is now the Guardian for the ward, it may be said that as an owner of property he has an ex- tensive interest as a ratepayer of Runcorn, and will un- doubtedly, look after the Interests of his fellow-rate- payers ; because they will to an extent, be identical with his own. This will be his first essay at public office, and judging from his personal abilities, he is likely to prove a trustworthy and painstaking public servant. —The last day for receiving withdrawals was Monday noon, but the retiring officer was acquainted with the candidates' intentions a little bore the final hour.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

Defeat has attended Warrington's first excursion to foreign soil, and defeat of the decisive nature of 9 points to nothing. Out of fuur matches W ,rriegtan have ben twice " oiled," and on each occasion their opponents have registered a trio of tries. The coincidence may ba curious out it is no: pleasing. La all probability a simi- lar result will follow the encounter with Salfard at New ' Barns, today. Warrington have bean for some time shocking performers beyond the limits of their own enclosure—and perhaps I might say within as well—but they do occasionally win at home, where ie away vict sry returns with them but twice or thrice a year. Widnes was one of the teanas t) be defeated away by War- rington last season, and no 029 was tilln surprised than the winners. Oa Saturday they avenged the insult and gave Warrington a fairly severe drubbing.

It has been re aorde I that in Widnes, all tie " Tired Titns " have to do for a rest is to carve out a cube of the bes,l atmosphere handy and sit upon it. Pneumatic cushions are pleasant luxuries, bat nothing in comparison with the Widnes air cushions. OPENING OF A NEW CEHETERY AT WIDNES.

The now cemetery provided by the Corporation of Widnes for that borough was formally opened on Tuesday afternoon by the M vor (Alderman J. W. Wareing), in the presence of the members of the Corporation, the public officials, and a large number of the public. The Mayor was accompanied 1)7 the Mayoress and the Misses Wareing. Among those present were Aldermen S. Sadler, J.P., Williamson, W. W. Gossage. J.P., and S. Quinn ; Councillors T. Sbuttleworth, W. Norrie, J. Mid- wood, S. Owens, J. Carroll, T. Carney, and T. Smith ; Messrs. J. S. Sinclair (borough surveyor and architect of the cemetery), T. °calk (deputy town clerk), &c..— Alderman Williamson, chairman or the Cemetery Com- mittee, on behalf cf the committee and the Council,asked the Mayor to open the gate oF the cemetery, and also presented his Worship with a handsome gold key. As rain had commenced te fall he did not offer any further reraarka ; and the Mayor was equally brief, simply ex- pressing the extrame gratification it afforded nun to perform that intevasting cerernoriy.—A tour of inspec- tion was then made of the cometery,wpich is situated at Moss Brook,Parnworth. . _

The necei'sit? of providing a public cemetery for the borough was first bfought under public notice in 1893, whea a committee V 734 appointed to report to the coun- cil on the subject. Tne committee ascertained that the Widnes Parish and the Mom an Catholic graveyards were almost full, and the council endorsed the finding of the committee by deciding that a public cemetery was accessary. It was resolved to purchase the MOS3Brook estate, consisting of 49 acre3,at a cost of £s,ooo,and the b irough surveyor (Mr. J. S. S nclair) was insirucite i to prepare the necessary plans for laying Oct 15 acres of the estate, and the constriction of three chapels. The cemetery has been divided into three rentions of almost equal proportion3.—Church of England, Nonconformists, and Roman Catholic. Ime three chapels are in the 13th century Gothic styie,constructed to suit modern require- ments. The Roman Catholic chapel stands by itself,and the Church of England and Nmconformists chapels are , combined, being joine I together by a handsome belfry spire. Etch cnapel has accommodatian for 60 persons, I and the arrangements are admirable in every respect. Entrance Co the cemetery is gained through an imposing Gothic arch, at the junction of Derby and Hirchfield roads. The erection of the chapels, registrar's lodge, an 1 th 3 entrance gate was carried out by Mr. Thomas Sadler, Widnes, and the whole of the work was designed by the borough surveyor. The cost of the ' buildings was £5,480 the lan.] £5,000, and altogether the cemet CiS5 about 213.030.—After the inspection the party returned to the 'Pawn Hall, where luncheon I was partaken of. Toe question of consecrating the Church of England potion of the cemetery his not yet been settled. The Free Church Culei!, on behalf of the N meonf has strongly opposed consecreation ; and the Uaited Church Councils of Widnes, Farnworth, arid Sli.Ambros parishes have decided to petition the council in favour of consacration.

LATEST NEWS. DEATH OF THE QUEEN OF DENMARK..

COPENHAGEN. THUESDLY.

The Queen of Denmark died at half past five this morning. Her Majesty passed away peacetu'ly. At her bedside were the King, the Dowager-Empress of Russia, the King and Queen of Greece, the Princess of Wales,the Duke and Duchess ef Cumberland, the Crown Prince and Princess of Denmark, Prince and Princess Waidemar of Denmark, the Crown Prince and Princess of Greece, Prince and Princess Charles of Denmark and several of the younger members of the Royal Family. Early this morning Her Majesty's decease was known in Copenhagen. The papers brought out extra (editions, all,even the most Radical publishing highly sympathetic articles. Everywhere flags ale at half mast. The Queen died not from any special illness, but of increasing decrepitude.

ST. PAUL'S CEIURCEL WIDNE3.

: On Sunday services were held, in connection with St. Paul's Church to celebrate the harvest festival. Tne various parts of the building were mo3t tastefully adorned with flowers and fru t. In the morning the pulpit was occupied by the Rev. C. R. Killick, M.A., of Runcorn. In the afternoon a children's service was conducted by the Rev. J. Adams. At the evening service the church was picked, fully 1,200 being present. The service was very finels, rendered. The Rev. W. Hodgson read the prayers, and Mr. Newberry the lessons. Upon this occasion

The Rev. W. Hodgson, Vicar of Widnes, preached his farewell sermon. He remarked that however much he might value it, he must not seek their praise or blame. He knew he had been guilty of sins of omission and com- mission. Be would say this, he had tried to do his duty day by day, and from morning to night. A steward must be fai:hful—he had tried to be—but in many ways he had failed. The Vicar of Widnes has an appaling task. He is supeosed to shepherd a parish of 19,000 people, and carry on the district work of two parishes. Either S. Mary's or St. Paul's would of itself tax the energies of an ordinary man. Every year the work gets heavie ,as the town gets poorer. From the first thing in the morning to the last thing at night, he had given himself to his work, but he had never been able to do it as he himself would have liked, let alone as his Master expected. Special harvest anthems and music were sung by the choir, the whole service being of a most im- lpre.ssive character. The offertory was greater than had ever been taken at an evening service. MOTHER curs HER CHILDREN'S THROATS.

Esrly yesterday morning Mrs. Scott, wife of Samuel Scott, labourer, Knocken, Oswestry, cut the throats of her four children. The woman was seen early in the morning by her sister, and shortly afterwards ran out shouting " I've done it, I've done for them." Two children are seriously injured. The womm and husband lived happily together,and there is nothing to account for the set, except that she has been depressed lately. THE NEW LORD MAYOR OF LONDON. At a meeting of the Liverymen and Aldermen of the City of London yesterday Sir John Voce Moore was ' unanimously elected ;Lord Mayor for the ensuing year. THE DISTRESS IN THE WEST INDIES. -SERIOUS RAILWAY ACCIDENT NEAR BARNSLEY.

Early Fester lay morning a serious accident occurred on the Midland main line near Houghton Main, iarfield, causing groat dimaite and s riOus delay to passenger traffic. The accident was caused by bullocks straying on the line frltn an adjoinihg field. A goods train from Sheffield to Normanton caught abe ist, which threw many trucks off the line, fouling the up-line. A goods train coming in the opposite direction dashed into the obstruction, the engine being overturned. No one was soriously injured. WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION AT BOLTON, HEAVY DAMAGES.

At Bolton on We inesday, his Honour Judge Jones awarded, under the Workmen's Compensation Act, £250 damages t 3 a joiner named Edwin Belly, aged thirty-two who had hem diFahled whilst working on a scaffold for Messrs. Marsden, of Bolton St Saw Mills,who admit- ted liability. Plaintiff alleged that he complained that the scaffold on which he was working was unsafe, but his warning was not heeded. His Honour said that irk such a case he was bound to award heavy damages. THE CHINESE CRISIS. CRITICAL CONDITION OF THE EMPEROR.

It is reported from Canton that the British Consul there has been instructed to protect the family of the Chinese ref orm leader, Kang-lru-Wei, now en route to long Bong from shanghai. The Pekin correspondent of the " Times" says :—An Imperial edict regrets the increasing ill-health of the Emperor, and commands the Governors of all provinces to send the best physicians to Pekin, thus preparing the people in case his Majesty's illaess should be aggravated. The death of the Emperor would now affect the situa- tion, the succession being already provided for.

THE SIRDAR AND RETIR,ENIENT.

The war correspondent of the "Daily Telegraph" in Cairo says :—lt is stited on good authority that the Sirdar has resigned or intends retiring at once from the Egyptian Army. He regards his work as accomplished, and will return to England. It is believed that Major- General Hunter will succeed him as Sirdar. It is re- ported that Captain Marohand objected to the raising of the English and Egyptian flags at Fashoda, bet his pro- test was disregarded, although the Sirdar treated the French party courteously. Captain Marchand salt that opposition was hopeless. A small Egyptian garrison was subsequently left south at Sobat.

Among the subjects taken are arithmetic, drawing, history, shorthand, and domestic economy. ANNIVERSARY SERVICES : —Sunday being the 60th anniversary of the church's consecration, special ser- vices were held morning and evening. The preacher at the morning service was the Vicar (Rev. .1. J. Langham) who gave a resume of parochial and church work 111 his parish during the last 60 years. In the evening the sermon was preached by th 3 Rev. Canon Willis, Rector of Warrington. The off N.:ooes which amounted to nearly £lO were given to the Vicar for the pecuniary loss he sustained on the abolition of pew ;outs last year. DEATH OF AN OLD INHABITANT.—OLI Sunday after- noon after a brief illness, death removed a well known and respected figure from this Parish in the person of Mr. Henry Antrobus (of Padgate-lane) at the advanced age of 87 years. His remains were interred .in Padgate Churchyard, on Wednesday. The officiating clergyman, was the Rev. W. R. Hartwright.

SPIRITIIA.LISM.

On Sunday and Monday the meetings of the Spiritualist Society were addressed by Mr. E. Mark- le w. On Sunday afternoon the address was based upon the words "Condemn not," and in the evening he took for his subject" Nature's divine revelations.' The Monday evening's meeting was held in the Druids' Hall, and after replying to a question from the audience, Mr. Marklew gave several clairvoyant delineations. BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN BAND OF HOPE.

An entertainment was given in the schoolroom on Monday evening in connection with the above, the chair being occupied by Councillor Bennett who delivered an address to the parents and children. The Rev. W. Potts also addressed the meeting. The programme was well selected. Miss Maud Davey gave a pianoforte solo in a manner which showed very careful training. A solo, " The Children's Home " by Miss Hettie Perkins was splendidly rendered ; also a solo, " The Gay Tomtit," by Miss E. Taylor, received an encore. Recitations were given by Miss E. Cross, Minnie Whitmore, and Mr. George Whitfield. The one by Mr. Whitfield, "The Factory Chimney," was delivered with pathos and power. Two mandoline solos by Miss Leek,of Fara- worth, Bolton, were highly appreciated. The chief item was a dialogue, entitled" The Flower Queen's Court,"the piece being taken by twelve girls dressed in white and weiriing. flowers to represent their parts. The Queen, Miss Maud Davey, was dressed in white and Wore a crown of beautiful white flowers, her train being carried by two little girls. Her representation of the Queen was perfection in every sense. All deserve equal praise for the man- ner in which they sustained their parts. The solos of the piece were sung by the Misses Emily Taylor, Gertrude Pucill,and Ada Smith. Mrs. F. Pendlebury again kiLdly acted as accompanist.

THE BENEFITS OF SCIENCE.

It is seldom that any discovery of fitience is such art will benefit all classes of people. Some of the greatest discoveries of the age, white they have proved directly beneficial to thousands, have also, at least for a time, Injured other thousands. Even some of the best medi- cinal preparations, however much they may do to re- move one class of disease, or suit some constitutioes, at the same time either deepen the hold of another disease, or leave it entirely untouched, or if they benefit one class of patients, they may injure another. Now the great need of the age is soma scientifically arranged preparation which will cope effectually with the preva- lent diseases of this country, which will be certain to de good when fairly tried,which will be equally adapted to the needs of the merchant and the workman, the pro- fessional man, and he who wins his bread by the sweat of his brow, the student, the clerk, the factory hand,the miner, and the roadside labourer.lt should be, too, such a preparation as contains no injurious ingredients, and which may be taken with impunity by the weakly child, or the delicate lady, as well as by the *stronger constitu- ted man. Such a discovery would deserve to be called The Perfection of !Medicinal Preparations, and would indeed be an Invaluable Boon to Suffering Humanity. Now this much needed boon has been found, has been tried, has been proved, and wherever proved,it has been found satisfactory. We refer to Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters, The Vegetable Tonic, which when once tried,has been always recommended, and has proved successful when all other medicice has failed to give relief, and we may say further, that it has proved permanently benefi- cial, when other preparations at best only gave tempor- ary relief. It is strongly recommended as The Best Remedy of the Age for Indigestion in its different forms, such as Sick Headache,Pains in the Side, Giddiness,Lose of Appetite,also for Nervousness and Nervous Disorders, Sleeplessness, Neuralgia, Low Spirits, and all kinds of Weakness. It has often proved very beneficial to persons suffering from great Weakness, either after an Illness, long confinement to ill-ventilated rooms, or any other cause. It strikes at the source of the Disease, removing the cause of the illness, and strengthens those parts of the system which have been weakemed by it, and therefore most liable to colds and other ailments. Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters being purely vegetable, it is a Natural Tonic, and suited to all ages at all seasons of the year. It is everywhere acknowledged to be a powerful aid in restoring the body to a healthy state when deranged by disease.lf you suspect that your health is beginning to fail, brace up your nerves, and fortify your constitution by taking now a course of this excel- lent Tonic, which is sold everywhere in bottles at 25.9 d. and 45.6 d. eacie,but should any difficulty be experienced in procuring it, the Proprietors will forward same for the above prices, carriage free. ALLEM) THEFT OF a GINGEH-BEE/1 Box. At the Borough Police Court, on Friday morning, be- fore the Mayor and other magistrates, Aloysius Hughes, 25, Pinners-brow, was charged with stealing a ginger. beer box, the property of Ellen Hinds on the 17th September. Defendant pleaded not guilty.—Mr.A.Browne who appeared for the prosecutrix said the pastes lived together for about seven years at one time, and when defendant was ill for about six months, the prosecutrix nursed him through it, and he afterwards turned her out. Dr. Fox who owned the property in Mersey-street, then turned Hughes out and let prosecutrix have the shop. On the day in question his client sent two boys out for a dozen of ginger-beer,and passing Hughes' shop, he sailed them in and took the box from thorn saying it was his. The box was his client's. He had a witness who would prove making the box for her. There was no name on it and defendant had his name stamped on all his.—Ellen Hinds, 35, Mersey- street, bore out her salicitor's opening statement. Some three or four months ago she ordered some boxes to be made, and the box in question was the sample one. When she saw it she said she would not have the other twenty made.—Joseph Leigh, cooper, 45, Mersey-street, proved making a box as a sample.—Mrs. Wright said she was in Mrs. Hinds' shop on Saturday last when the boys were sent for the ginger teer.—Henry Carrington. 49, Wilderspool-road. said he with another boy named John Forster carried the box.—Mr. Hughes called them in kis shop as they passed and took the box off them and told them to tell Mrs. Hinds that if she bad any more of his boxes she would get into trouble. They would fine him in the costs, liimply 103.

PROPRIZTOS.

At the Warrington Borough Police Court on Wednes- day before Mr. R. T. Fairclough (in the chair) and other magistrates, James Barrow, Thomas Barrier, and Wil- liam Smith, (all young men) were charged with srealiog from a field in Winwick-road the previous day about 3 o'clock, three geese the property of George Browis,value 15s.—Defendants all pleaded not guilty.—The Chief Constable said Mrs. Brown was the wife of George Brown of 303 Wiawick-road. The two arisoners Barrow were brothers and lodged with a Mrs. Dunn at 111 Winwick-road. Mrs. Brown saw her geese safe about 230 and missed them shortly after 3 o'clock. Mrs. Dunn saw her two lodgers come home accompanied by the prisoner Smith. They had been drinking and neglecting their work for the day and she left Ithem all in the house. Shortly after throe o'clock she returned and Mrs. Brown in formed her of her loss. She went into her own house- and noticed a quantity of dirt and feathers. She said to Thomas Barrow" have those geese been in here." He said "no I have not seen them." Mrs. Brown and Bar- row then went upstairs where the other two men were. She saw more feathers and a quantity of blood." She said to the men, " you have killed those geese." They protested they knew nothing about them, and then went out of the house. She met Mr. Johnson Wilson with a cab and told him what had occurred. He followed the men as far as Bewsey Bridge, and then hurried into the town and meeting Sergeant Dean and P.C. Whittaker , west back and the men were arrested. rhe two officers then went back to the house where the men lodged. They found the three missing geese, one with its head off Thomas Barrow said " I killed them, the other two did ' not kill them." He need not point out that the whole three were responsible for the actions of the one. Mrs. Brown and Mrs •Dunn igave evidence which bore out the Chief Constable's statement.—Johnson Wilson, nab proprietor, Warrington, said he was in Bewsey-road about 4 o'clock when Mrs. Dunn called him to the pri- soners and made a communication to him. He followed them and they were subsequently arrested.—Sergeant Dean said the prisoner Thomas Barrow said to him "I killed th 3 geese and the others had nothing to do with it." Thomas Brown said " We are all three as bad as one another." At the police station Thomas Barrow said to James" You keep your mouth shut it is no use us all three being implicated in it."—P.C. Whittaker proved finding the geese. Thomas Barrow's hands were covered with dirt and blood.—The Chief Constable said he must point out that there was no consistency in their repeated denials of any knowledge of the theft, but might he suggest that as they were young men of previ- ous good character, a fine be inflicted. Ho looked upon it as a drunken freak.—The Chairman said they con- sidered the prisoners were alrguilty of the charge, but as they thought it a drunken freak they would fine them a guinea including costs or a month with hard labour. The Chairman then said they were very much obliged to Mr. Johnson Wilson for the way in which he had con- ducted himself in regard to the prisoners. It reflected great credit upon him—The Chief Constable added his thanks on behalf of the police, to those of the bench.

JEWELS IN VELVET USES.

IN a case lined with silk or velvet, there is where the lady who has diamonds is apt to keep them. Not be- cause they are likely to suffer from exposure. Oh, no, a diamond can eta nd anything in the line of weather, and any amount of rough usage. But it is small and Costly; easy to loss and hard to find ; and much desired by those gentlemen who, like Mr. Niternmick, are fond of portable property. In the body, which is the most delicate and beauti- ful of velvet cases, nature keeps those jewels, the vital organs. Unlike diamonds these precious things do not well endure exposure and careless treatment. Especi- ally are they injured by co!d. Therefore, en far as. Possible, they are surrounded by warm fiesta and pro- tented from chance shocks or Wows from without. For- tunately for their owner no thief covets them, as, once taken from the case, they have no value. What we may call the Koh-i-noor among these living gems is the liver. It occupies the warmest and cosiest nook of the body, well sheltered from cold. Yet it is not thereby made secure from evil. Its gravest dangerc come from the other jewels in the case, and from the Owner who has power over them all. Now 1-t us see what these facts have to do with the cases of Mr. Robert Lee and others—you and me perhaps among them. "In November, 1895," writes Mr. Les, " I began to get weak and low. My appetite was poor and the little food I ate gave roe a deal of pain about tha chest. I was unable to sleep well at night,and in the morning felt exhausted and tired."

Mr. Lee's statement ;that he was " tirel " after ling on his bed all night is physiologically correct. Fatigue is lois of strength whether from labour or any other cause. You will note, however, that while fatigue arising from healthy exertion i 9 overaome by food and sleep, this kind cannot be overcome save by curing the disease which produces it. " My feet," continues Mr. Lee, " were swollen and painful, and a tendency to gout manifested itself. I felt low-spirited and thought I was breaking up. Presently I became so weak 1 cOuld scarcely move about. Month after month I remained in this condition. Having heard so much about the efficacy of Mother Seigel's Syrup in complaints of this kind,l began using it,and soon found benefit. I could eat well, and food agreed with ma. I continued to take it arid improved from week to week. It was not long before the gouty sympto me entirely disappeared, arid I was in my usual good health. Should you desire to publish this letter you may feel free to do so."—(Signed) Robert Lee, Postmaster, Tealby,Market Rasea, Lincolnshire, Feb. 22nd, 1898. Whether Mr. Lee is or not subject to gout, and has, therefore, had experience of it, he does not tell us. In any case he will please take notice that the immediate cause of gout is what is technically called reversion of the liver, allowing the formation in the blood of uric acid, a poison which, settling in the joints, creates the inflammation and the pain; while the partial inaction of the kidneys causes the bloating. Be ant others who have suffered in the same way will be wise to .look sharply aft3r those " goety symptoms." For gout is not an ailment all by itself ; it is a member of a very dangerous combination, which may include affections of the heart, skin, nerves, brain, bladder and kidneys._ -

. . The whole troub!e begins, as so many troubles do, in the stomach. Digestion, to a greater or leis degree, fails ; fermentation of food in the digestive organs follows ; the bowels become c estive ; the rotting sub- ,tances r Amain in the stomach and intestines ; the blood is filled with impurities ; the over-worked liver re- fusel its duty ; the change from food to body-tisaue is not completed ; the deadly uric acid is formed in dan- gerous qasotities, and several diseases, af chiOh gout is one, ve set golov. To correct this state of things is always diffilult and o'ten impossible. _

Ths prop ir treatment is, by the aid of Mothar osigel's Syrup, to puree the blood, and indesd the whole sysi,sm of the fearful corruption engendsred within it, and to tone Vie domicil, liver, skin, bowels and ki inns to act powerfully and naturilly. The Syrup may be relied on to do this even when other remedies hive Sally fallen short. And for us all the Mason is this:—Cure indigestion in it; earli staves by taking the Syrup when the first sign; of it appear.

LANCASHIRE SENIOR COMPETITION. WA.RRINGTON y. WIDNES.

THE " CHEMICALS " STILL UNBEATEN.

Warrington undertook one of the stiffest tasks they have to face this season, when they journeyed to Widnes on Saturday. The " Chemicals" have come on wonder- fully this last two seasons, and are one of the most for- midable combinations in the League at present, whereas their neighbours at Warrington have been slowly but surely deteriorating. Though Warrington succeeded in beating the Widnesians on their own soil at the close of last season, there were few who thought them capable of spading the homesters' unbeaten record on Saturday. Warrington had a lot in their favour, for Widnes had to find substitutes for Brennan and Alec Martin, in the three-quarter line, whereas Warringten were only short of R. Bate, for whom Ashurat made an excellent substi- tute. The weather was bright and cool, being favour- able to spectators and players alike. Of the former there were about 5,000, something like 1,000 coming from Warrington. Mr. J. Priestley (of Broughton) was referee, and the players lined out as follows : Widnes : Batey, full back; Peters, Rispan, Taylor, and Peacock, threequarters; Drummond and Douglas, half backs; Heslop, Rampson, Hunt, Handley,Johnson, Travis, &spay, and Donnelly forwards. Warrington: BOiC3W, full back; Holcroft,Duckwerth, I-herwood, and Moores. threequarters ; J T Bate and Ashurst, half-backs ; Taylor, Deakin, Naylor, Barratt, Boardman, Eden, Fairhurst, and Sudlovr, forwards. Taylor started for Warrington with a slight breeze in his favour, and no return being made, the first scrim- mage occurred in Widoes territory. The homesters got possession and Douglas kicked, but the ball was lost in touch. Isherwood made a fine mark from a clever punt by Peters, and play ruled even. Bate and Moores en- deavoured to make the running, but Peacock floored the latter with great promptness. A lofty and well-judged kick by Douglas gave the borne front rank a fine chance, and the famous forwards looked like rushing over when a knock-on caused the intervention of the referee's whistle. A lough punt by Batey forced 503C0W over the line, where be mulled, but in the nick of tune sent the ball dead. Bate and Ashurst relieved, Boardman spoiling what looked like a chance by an unlucky kick which sent the ball in touch well within the Widnes quarter. Moores was prominent with some tricky play and Widnes had to defend. The home front rank however, soon removed the danger to the other end, and Taylor effected a daring gave for Warrington. Bats made a couple of useful marks from long punts by Bate'', who was playing a great game. Warrington were hard pressed, and " Smack " endeavoured to kick the ball to Moores, but seat it backwards and Boscow had all his work cutout in the corner. Here Widnes made a great effort and se- curing the ball Drummond gave Peacock a quick pass and the latter rushed over. The place-kick was a little too difficult for Batey,who made a good attempt. War- rington made little headway after this,and Bosoow being tackled in possessiea of the ball, Widnes attacliel strongly. Duckworth did several smart things for War- rington. Aspey,of Willies, was caught in the act of taking a kick at Bate and the referee warned him and took his name. Just before the interval Warrington had another good opening, but Fairhurst just did what Boardman had done before, and the chance was lost. Half-time came with the score, Widnes one try, War- rington nil. _

Heslop recommenced hostilities, and a grand run by Isherwood into the home half was the first item of nate. A trout of exchange kicking left the game waging at centre, but before five minutes hid elapsed, Duckworth I had his kick charged down, Boscow was too slow to get to the ball, and ['estop dribbled over with an easy try. Batee failed again at the place kick. Play proceeded in the Warrington half until Ashurst relieved by the aid of a free-kick. From a mark by Taylor, (Widnes), Batey tried a shot from centre which fell short, the ball being well returned by Isherwood. A forward rush by Widnes troubled Boscovr, who was downed by Hampson, and WarrMgton had an anxious time. A short kick in front of the posts sent the ball to Bascow who secured it, but immediately lost possession and presented Travis with a I very soft try. Though the place was not difficult, Heslop was unsuccessful at goal. Warrington now lost heart, ;.though they defended stubbornly. Bate made one or two desperate efforts, but was always brought down in short order. Warrington made occasional excursions to the home line, Bate. Ashurst. and Isherwood passing well en one ocoasion. Then Moores looked a likely scorer,but Widnes defended grandly. Holcroft had a penalty in good position bet made a poor attempt. Widnes rushed the game to the other ond,Moores saving in fine style. Rispan had hard luck with a drop at goal, and shortly after the whistle sounded finally, :leaving Widnes winners by 3 'tries (9 points) to Warrington, nil. OLDPIAM v. RUNCORN.

A ST. HELENS MAN'S PRICARIOLTS CONDITION. William Cunliffe, a middle-aged man, of 71, Bolton. street, St. He'ens, an employee at Messrs. Quirk,Barton, and Co.'s leadworks, was admitted to the Providence Hospital on Sunday in a serious condition, suffering from broken ribs, injured lungs, and other injuries. Cunliffe's condition was also aggravated by an attack of bronchitis. It is stated that Cunliffe's ribs were broken by a fall, but considerable mystery sur- rounds the acedent, which happened on Saturday. Cunliffe is in a dangerous condition. REMARKABLE DESERTION CASH AT ST. HELENS, At St. Helens on Monday. Mrs. Charlotte Pennington summoned her husband for desertion. Mrs. Pennington sail that six years ago her husband deserted her, and went to work as a collier at Burnley. In answer to Mr. E. W. Swift, who appeared for the husband, Mrs. Pen- rington admitted that during the time her husband had b-en away she had had a child by a lodger. She only wanted some contribution towards the maintenance of Pennington's three children. The bench said they could not make an order after the woman's admission, but Mr. Swift undertook that the man would contribste to be support of the children. A DETERMINED ST. HELENS BUTCHER. James Fairolouzh, butcher, of 28, Kirkland-street was charged at St. Helens on Monday with wilfully damaging the ironwork of the tramway line in Westfield-street From the evidence of John Sheridan, a Corporation watchman, and other witnesses, it appears that between eleven and twelve o'clock on the night of Wednesday last Fairclough drove up to the top of Westfield-street. The whole of the road surface had been taken up, and Sheridan told him that there was no possibility of getting down the street. Fairclough said he would find a road or make it. He drove through the barrier and down the tram lines, which were quite exposed, no pavement being between thorn. In driving down the road Fair- clone' damaged the tie-rods between the rails and generally upiet the work, doing three guineas worth of damage.—Fairclough admitted that he drove down the street.—He was fined £1 and costs.

ACCIDENT TO POLICE INSPECTOR MORGAN. Inspector Morgan is at present confined to his house as the result of an accident which he met I with on Monday night. He was making his way 'home about midnight across the Market ground, , when he caught his foot in an open drain pipe and I fell. He was unable to rise, and had to remain on ithe ground until P.C. Aspden came by and went to the station house for assistance. He was then carried home, and Dr. Patterson was summoned. He fownd on examination that Mr. Morgan had very severell sprained his ankle, and states it will be at least three weeks before he will ba able ta get about again. THE SUDDEN DEATH AT NEWTON. The namo of the man who was found dead in Aehton-road, Newton on Thursday last week, was found to be Hugh Spencer, a day wage man of 281 Church-road, New Boston, Haydock. He had been suffering for the past four years from heart disease, in fact ever since he left the Army, and had been attended by Dr. Hayward. During the summer he had been an inmate of the Miners' Hospital where he had been treated for the complaint. He had practically only just left the hospital, and the doctor had certified him as fit for work. On the day he died he had been to the surgery of Dr. Watkins, Newton, for medicine for one of his chil- dren, and was returning home, when death over- took him.

MANCHESTER. Two local veterans, Mr. C. Owen, and Mr. John Hazledin, of Earlesto wn, attended the eat ertainmeut which was given to old Crimean soldiers at Belle Vue, Manchester, on Thursday, under the presi- dency of Lord Roberts. Mr. John Hazledin saw 'plenty of service in the Crimea, being present at Ithe battle of Alma, and at the siege and fall of ISebastopol. He was a private in ths 95th Darby- shire Regiment of foot, and spent three months in the trenches before Sebastopol, and was wounded in the leg. After the fall of Sebastopol he spent three months in the place, forming a member of the garrison. Mr. Owen was with the Baltic fleet before Sebastopol, and was landed with the naval rigade during the storming of the place. SUDDEN DEATH OF MRS. DUN, EARLESTOWN. She took to her bad where she remained all day on eriday and despite every attentiin ohe died on Saturday morning at 10 o'clock, from an in- ternal malady. LIGIITING.

A special meeting of this -Council was held in the Town Hall, Earlestown, on Monday afternoon, to consider the advisability or otherwise of apply- ing to the Board of Trade for a Provisional Order empowering the Council to supply electricity for lighting and other purposes within the district, under the Electric Lighting Acts, and to pass such resolutions in connection therewith as the Council may then and there determine.—Mr. C.l) B. F. Borron, J.P„ presided, and there were present : Council:ors Stone, Emmett, Ormaad Evans, Gil- mour, Thompson, Patterson. Wood, Mcnah-m, Simpson, Beatty and Forster.—The Chairman stated that the question of Electric Lighting had been carefully considered by the Committee and an esti- mate of the cost had been submitted and con- siderei, he thereupon moved, and it was seconded by Mr. Thompson and unanimously resolved—That, having considered the advisability of applying to the Board of Trade for a Provisional Order empowering the Council to supply electricity for lighting and other purposes within the district, under the Elec- tric Lighting Acts, the whole question be deterred.

ALLEGED STABBING AT GOLBORNE. At the Warrington Olcasbnal Court on Monday morning, before Mr. S. R. Duffey was unable to be present owing to the in- juries received---John Towey, John-street, Golborne said he was in John-street at ten p.m., on the 24th September, when he heard a row going on in prisoner's house. He stood in front of the house and saw prisoner's husband strike Duffey, who re- taliated. He saw the orisoner come from the back kitchen and strike Duffey on the bead three or four times. He could not tell what she had in her hand. Duffey came out of the house and said" She has stabbed me." He leaned against him, and witness saw blood flowing from his face. Duffey then fell to the gr)und, and he was carried into his own buse by three men. Prisoner's husband had hold of Duffey at the tins, be was struck. There was no one else standing near the door but himself.—P.C. Wild, stationed at Golborne, said that from informa- tion received, he went to Duffey's house and found him sitting on a chair suffering from a cut in the nose. _ - UNIVERSITY EXTENSION LECTURES AT ST. HELENS. The first rf a nurse of six lectures on tt The Puritan Revolution," arranged by the S. Helens Corporation Technical Education Committee in connection with the Oxford University Extension Lectures scherne,was given in the St. Helens Town Hall, on Tuesday night, by Mr. G. C. Henderson, 8.A., of Bal:i'd College, Oxford. Not- withstanding the incl.ment weather there was a good attenclance,and the M yor (Aid. R.Pilkington) presided 'rhe lecturer dealt interestingly with the events and con- troversies which lei up to the expulsion of Puritan clergy from the church in 1605', and the subsequent struggles which hod been called the Puritan Revolution. NOTES BY THE WA-k- AT EARLESTOW.N.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT.] The District Council have had a special meeting this weak, and have decided to defer the applica- tion for a Provisional Order for the electric lighting of the township. his means, I suppose, that the question is " shelved " for good, and that the private gentleman, who has practically introduced the sub- ject can proceed with his application without fear of interference from the Council. Of course, I don't know whathas prompted the Council to drop th 3 electric lighting, unless it is upon the question of expense. I thought we were getting aristocratic too rapidly.

One hears little or nothing about the water ques- tion now, though if you take a walk as far as Low- ton, you will see the boring apparatus at work, but that is all. 'Tis precious little water you will see, and you will not be able to gat to know with any degree of accuracy, what the expectations are in the direction of ultimate succesi. It is about time we knew something. Either the apparatus is not of the best, or the ground is not of the softest to enable the boring to proceed with any approach to speed. However, as in all undertakings of this kind, I suppose we must be patient. "Everything comes to him who waits."

The fire which broke out on Mr. Bridge's farm a fortnight ago could not satisfactorily be accountel for,but various reasons w-re put forth,any of which might be feasible. It might have arisen from spon- taneous combustioa, which is the mot likely, and it might have been caused by other means. Give a lad a box of matches and there is no telling what might happen. At Collins Green this week some lads were playing near a stack of hay, and one of them produced a box of Bengal lights, which he procEeied to ignite. They give out a strong coloured light,and to heighten the effect, his companions as- sisted in throwing the lights in the air. Unfortu- nately one of the lighted matches fell on the stack, and immediately there was a blaze. The owner hap- pened to be on the spot and he promptly extin- guished the fire, but what would have happened if he had not been there? The lads, when they saw the mischief, would have cleared off and left the stack to its fate. This is one way of causing a con- flagration.

===== 0002601_18980930_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====

NEWTON ADJOURNED LICENSING SESSIONS.

The sessions were held at the Court House Newllon-12- Willowg on Thursday. Colonel Wood presided.

A WINE LICENSE GRANTED.

Mr. E. A. White. said he appeared on behalf of Joseph Michael Rogers to anply for a wine license for the Sir Charles Napier Hotel, Gslborne. His application was a molest one ; and in addition there was no opposition. Tnere was reasonable requirement for toe license and the street in which the Hotel was situated was the principal street of the town. It was High-street. There were four rooms ddwnstairs and six upstairs. Adjoining the premises there was stabling for ten horses. The house belonged to the Bedford Beer and Maltin Company. The applicant had held the license for eighteen months,during which time no complaint had been made against him. The police reported his charac- ter to be good and that was the best evidence their Worships could have. The house was used very much as a ref reshment houge,and a wine license was regarded generally as very useful with refreshment horses. If the wine licence was granted a husband and wife would be more likely to go into tbe houie together, which in his opinion would los a move towards temperance. There was a large body of men otherwise teetotal who drank wine. They wrre not teetotal in the strict sense of the word ; but hs would submit they were entitled Co con- sideration as well as any other class. _

Joseph Michael lbgers, licensee of the Sir Charles Napi-r Hotel, High-street,Golborne, said High-street was the principal street in Colborne. He believed if the license was granted he woul Ibe able to extend the r 3 treatment business.

FATAL FALL AT BA.NIC QUAY STATION.

INQUEST AND VERDICT.

On Saturday evening an accident occurred to Job Wardle, 31 years of age, briclrsetten labourer, 50. Sharp- street, Warrington, which ended fatally. The &ceased had seen his sister off by t'ee 5 10 p.m. train from War- rington to Daresbury, and when leaving the station by the stone steps fell, and his head struck one of the bot- tem steps, tie was unconscirms from the fall and was conveyed to the Infirmary, but died without regaining consciousnees.

The inquest was held at the Police Parade Room on Monday morning, before Mr. S. Brighouse. coroner.— Ann Wardle, widow, said deceased went out of the house about 3 o'clock with his brother's wife. He was going to the station. She did not see him alive again. Decvased was sober when he went out.—James Plinston, ticket collector, Bank Quay Station, said he saw de ceased passing the barrier to go to the piatforin at 4.b5. He might have had a little drink but not much IHe was accompanied by a lady. He next saw I him about 5.15. He heard a fall and saw some porters ,Igathered at the bottom of the steps. There was a crowd at the time, awl when he got there deceased was lying, in an unconscious condition. Ho was tak mto the In- firmary. There was a hand-rail down the steps at each side. The steps were in good condition, and no similar accident had occurred for 16 years.—Thomas Walsh, a post office employ, said he went to the station at five minutes to five to meet the 5 3 train. When the train drew up alongside the platform he saw deceased and a woman. The latter got in the train. Deceased then welt down the steps. He held the rail with both hands as he went down. When he got to the middle lanling, be left bold as if he thought be was at the bottom of the steps. He seemed to stagger then turned half round and fell backwards.—Nellie Isherwood, nurse at the Infir- mary, said de-Tased was admitted at a quarter past five. He died at 7.30 from a fracture of the base of the skull. The jury returned a verdict of " Accidental death."

WARRINGTON CHESS AND DRAUGHTS CLUB. The Warrington Chess and Draughts Club will commence the season on Monday next at 7 o'clock. The club room is at Mr. G. H. T3ft's Restaurant, 5, Buttermarket-street. Club nights Monday and Friday. The committee would urge each member to nominate at least one new member in order to increase the prospects of the club.

MDBIC IN TEA PARK. The last band performance of the season in the Park was Riven on Sunday, when there was again a large attendance. The music was supplied by the League of the Cross Band. The audiences this season have been larger than in previous years,and the performances have been greatly appreciated. There will be no pertormance by the massed bands this year. Two sermons were preached in the above chapel on Sunday by Mr. John Wright,of Glazebury. suit- able hymns and anthems were sung by the choir under the able leadership of Mr. J. if. Cooke. The solos were ably rendered by Miss Howard and Miss Millington, Mr. Albert Trow, Mr. Ryder, and Mr. Scott. Mr. David Hickman ably presided at the organ. In the afternoon a sacred concert took ' place. Mr. Hannan sang "The Day of Redemp- tion," with great taste. Mr. Albert Trow and Mr. Bates gave a selection on pianoforte and violin and both excelled themselves. The Sisters Eves from Padgate also played selections on pianoforte and violin. Mr. Albert Trow was the accompanist on the pianoforte, and Mr. Charles Simpson occupied the chair. Mr. Wright, of Glazibury, delivered abort address to the young men and women, and scholars connected with the school. Afterwards the President of the P.S.A. (Mr. J. J. WARRINGTON DISTRICT. In response to an invitation about twenty-five members, including the District Secretary, T. Dick- inson, and District Deputy-Grandmaster, John Bur- gess, visited the Manchester district on Saturday afternoon. On arrival at the city they were met by Bro. James Dwane, gran lmaster ; Bro. James Myers, district treasurer: and Bro. John H. Efewert- lson, district secretary,who conducted them to their headquarters, the Cheshire Cheese Hotel,Bridge-st., Deansaate, where they received a hearty welcome by other Manchester brethren. They were after- wards conveyed by 'bus to Cheadle and back to the Cheshire Cheese, where a capital tea had been pro- vided. After tea short speeches were delivered by Bros. Myers, Hewertson, Dwane, Platford, and Hodge (Manchester), Bros. Dickinson, Burgess, Nevins, and Bramhall (Warrington), interspersed with songs and recitations by various members of the districts. After passing votes of thanks the social gathering was brought to a close to allow the visitors to catch the 10.50 train home. PREMIHR CYCLES,"speelal terms.—GABLaTID, Agent Warrington* ACCIDENTS TO CYCLISTS. On Saturday two Manchester cyclists, riding down the Penny Hill, Lyman, which is marked to ride with caution, were both unable to control their Imachines, and see;ng the further dip past the church to the Dam Head turned their machines into the walls of the Masonic Hall rather than face the unknown further slope. Art eye-witness says they both came a terrific smash against the build- ing, and he thought they would be killed. They were picked up bleeding and stunned and their machines were damaged to a great extent. Mr. Hooley, of the Fleece Hotel, put them in a room by themselves, and gave them every attention until they were able to be assisted to the train. Near the High Legh C. T. C. Farm on Sunday night a Crewe cyclist, who had been to Liverpool, came a cropper owing to his machine collapsing, and broke his collar bone. His friends took him to Dr. Lead- better, at Latchford, who set the bone, and the party then went home by train from Warrington. A lady coming from Liverpool on Sunday night collided with another cyclist near Bold, and was upset, but fortunately got off with slight bruises. DROWNED /N THE SANKEY CANAL—INQUEST.

At the Chapel House Inn, Great Sankey, On Mon- day morning, an inquest was conducted by Mr. S. Brighouse touching the death of an unknown Per- son whose body was found floating in the Sankey and St. Helens Canal near the Eight Arches. on Friday morning.—John Sankey, anima Loch, Win- wick, stated that on the 23rd September he saw a body floating in the canal at Sankey. With assis- tance he got it to the bank, and found it was that of a woman,whom he thought had been in the water for about a week.—P.C.Dugdale,stationed at Sankey said that from information received he went to the Sankey and S. Helens Canal, where he found deceased lying on the towing-path, as described by the last witness. She appeared to be a woman of about 40 years of age. She had not been identified. He found 2s. 5d., in money in her picket, and a pip., a small piece of tobacco, and a piece of soap. She appeared to he a tramp. Thera was also a pawnticket upon her, which had been taken out on the 6th September. at Michael Fitzsimmon'e shop, 30, Scotland-road,LiyerpooLin the name of A. Read. I—P.S. Devlin said that inquiries had been made at the pawnshop, but nothing could be ascertained respecting her. He thought the woman had been a tramp, and that he had seen her before in the dis- trict.—A verdict of "Found drowned.' was returned. - EXCELSIOR and NEW ROWE CYCLES—GARLAND Iron,;onger. That debt still remained, but they wanted the money so that the churchwardens could pay their way and have a balance if possible, on the right side at Raster. They only wanted a small sum of money on the present occasion. The churchwardens would be happy if they got £5O. As there was going to be a very important bazaar in November in connection with the Parish Church. he thought the kindest thing they could do would be to buy articles at the sale of work and transfer them to the Rector for his bazaar. WATERS.—Advt. THE TEMPERANCE ELECTORAL PATRIOTIC UNION.

At the weekly meeting of Warrington i;;;;;;i; of the above union, held in the Temperance Hall, Howley, on Friday evening last week, the first an- niversary of its institution in Warrininon was commemorated. Bro. G. IL W. Steel, C.P. presided, and there were also present Bro. J. C. Morton,S.C.P., Bro. Ti. G. Brandreth, V.C.P. (who occupied the vice-chair), and Bro. Vickrag,e, P.T. After the routine business, the Chairman said they all felt extremely proud of having attained the anniversary of their institution. They had at their commencement 50 members ; they had now 105. They had much to congratulate themselves upon. They had a good hall to meet in, they had the inter- est and support of such ardent temperance workers as Bro. John Naylor, He.?., and Bra. J. C. Morton, S.C.P. To them they owed a great deal of their. present success. Bro. Morton was to them a guide counsellor, and friend, and were it not for the time, trouble, and ability he constantly bestowed upon them they would not hold the proud position they did that day. They had much to be thankful for, and he trusted it would spur them on to further and renewed efforts, and that the year they had just entered upon would be far more successful than the one they had ended, and that the fort would make its influence for good greatly felt in the district. Bro. J. C. Morton, S.C.P., spoke at length upon the good work which was being carried on by the fort in the Howley district, and hoped that the members would give the fort their support. Whatever he (the speaker) had done for the tem- perance cause had been out of a desire to benefit his fellow men. He hoped that before the next twelve months they would have another branch of the Union in Warrington. After the speeches an enjoyable entertainment was provided by Sister Morris and Brother Morton,Brothers Steel and Mor- rn, and Sister Breyshaw. Sister Brayshaw pre- sided at the piano.
