At the Westminster Pollee Court, on Tuesday. Luke James Alderson Peak, who had for twelve years beea political secretary to the Devonshire Club, was committed for trial on a charge of attempting to obtain 25 by means of worthless cheques. Great damage to property and some loss of life has been caused by a cyclone in part of the Niagara Peninsula. The release bus been ordered of Timothy Feattiorstone,a Star Bthnund O'Brien Kennedy. who was sentenced to penal servitude for life at the Liverpool Assizes in 1883 for treason felony at Liverpool. The American whaling fleet fishing in the waters of Beh- ring Straits is reported to have been crushed in the ice. The cmws are believed to have been taken off be other ship.. A liciadrld telegram states that the remains of Columbus will be conveyed from Havana te Spain by the steamer 310a- terele.

rac iur a surly uastitian's teeth,- and i snapped my fingers at him, for I was more disconcerted by Lucia's piercing eyes than by Muro's brag. "The despised bantam might as well try to peck the stars from the sky, or attempt to blow back a hurricane," he exclaimed, grinning villainously. "as think to annoy me." Lucia smiled and tossed her head. "The de'il tak' ye," I bluntly retorted, dropping the Spanish in ray wrath, "an' a' who aid an abet ye." 'Muro's fingers nervously twitched about the haft of his knife, and my hand went involuntarily to my derringer. Lucia stood between us, for she knew I was a good shot. The big foreigner bowed with ironical politeness, saying, with the air of a fine gentleman: "I measure the master, not the pup. This stung me, and I limped forward with - desperate resolve to make the Senorita move aside. ' But she tenaciously stuck to her post, extended her hands towards me, and cried in a harsh and imperious tone: "Enough! Sett ie this quarrel another day, when gin not present." "Then let this magnificent boaster," said I cynically, "withdraw his imputations." "This little bantam is game," he hoarsely ; sneered, grinding his even set of teeth, "but I'll cut his comb by-and-bye. Remember, Sandy Dees, a Castilian never forgets an insult." I scowled as a poacher •:caught trespassing, and quick-witted Lucia took advantage of my dilemma. She gasped a few words in my antagonist's ear. He shrugged his rshoulders, veered round, and swaggered away, whistling a Spanish melody. It is sometimes wise to pocket a rebuff, and so I bit my lip. To curb any mutinous spirit in me, which might rise to avenge my wounded pride, Lucia advanced, toying with her fan and smiling her sweetest. She eyed me from foot to crown, and said playfully: "I compliment you on your chivalry." I was so furious over this cutting sneer that my throat contracted and my reply was choked in its conception. "You are not loyal to Mark Pepys," I stam- mered at length. My facial expression and general condition of impotent rage were mirrored in Lucia's glistening eyes. I could see she was bravely striving te conquer her agitation. Are you paid to watch me ? " she asked haughtily, a blush suffusing her beautiful face and a tantalising smile playing about her pretty lips. "Mark Pepys is absolutely ignorant of my move - merits. 1 have simply a watch-dog's roving Commission." A sigh of relief escaped her, and looking stead- fastly, I saw tears welling to her eyelashes. "I am glad you confess this, she just managed to articulate; but immediately her sparkle and vivacity returned, and she added: 'I see your self-imposed task has sent you sprawling in the mud." This biting sarcasm disconcerted me for moment; but I think I looked terribly malicious, for the colour left the Senorita's cheeks. "No gentleman would play the spy upon a lady," she Plaintively added. "You fawn and flatter your lover, and sell him , —a worthy rile for an honourable lady!" MI ironical thrust wounded her, for she winced; and after a moment's awkward silence she cast a scorn- ful look at me and turned to go. But I barred the way, bearing the pain of my sprained ankle. The demon in her was roused. "You lie!" she hissed, excitedly beating the palm of her hand with her closed fan. 'Let me pass, or I will expose you as an unprincipled rogue!" She dashed past me, as I stood dumbfounded; and half - turning, her lips drawn, her cheeks flushed, and her eyes flashing anger, she added: "My ancestors broke spies upon the wheel. Beware! If you interfere with me again, your comb shall be cut and your wings clipped." This second edition of Muro's threat reminded me of a trump card. I steadied myself for retalia- tion, and pulling the gold cross from my breast, in a frenzy I jumped forward and held it before her face. She sprang back like a startled fawn, and pressed her throbbing bosom. "You have said bitter things," I madly exclaimed. "It is my turn now, Senorita! This cross is yours —you recognise it? Ah! your white and anxious face tells me so. You call me a spy; I say re are a cheat! You helped us to escape that Moro might strike deeper!" A thousand times afterwards I despised myself for uttering those words. Fear was unmistakably written on her brow, her lips twitched, she pressed her heart as if to ease a cutting pain. "Yes, the cross is mine, she gasped as I thought—and staggered back a pace or two. Then she brushed away her tears, and with wonder- ful self-control said calmly : "It is merely a keep- sake." —unguardedly, How apt are impetuous men to make fools of , themselves! Like a proud Pharisee I smote my breast, saying: "I'm no puppet dancing to your tune, nor a marionette for you to pull the strings!" Her face now flushed with scorn, she trembled with anger; so I added composedly, and with an air of injured innocence: "You misunderstand my motive in watching you and Muro." "Most generous knight - errant! " this with superabundant scorn. "This cross has left a trail of human blood.' I shot at a venture. "I guess I know the treacherous hand." She stood as though rOoted to the spot, wonder- ing what evidence I could adduce. I broke the silence. "It is a further proof of the double part you play." She shuddered, placed her fingers upon het temples, and breathed convulsively. Then with n long-drawn sigh she said pathetically: "This is more painful to me than you imagine, and for reasons that I cannot give. In your desire W serve Mark Pepys, you place him in peril." "Bah! A subterfuge." I retorted rebelliously. She shook her head, smilingly delicately, for I j affected a worse humour than I felt. "Through lack of judgment," she continued. ; somewhat sadly, "you may bring great sorrow upon him. As for me, I am convinced you have not the heart to persecute a woman. I implore you to desist. Because you fail to admire Mark's choice, you have no right to suspect her. There are man/ things we have to bury in our own bosoms, and none so good that we should condemn on mere outward appearance." "Senorita, I never mince matters. I'm loyal to the man who befriended me. If danger threatens him, I tell you candidly, I shall strive with all my heart and soul to avert it." My frankness pleased Lucia, for she smiled, and holding out her hand said winningly: "May I hare my own ? " "Since Queen liana's people are so exclusive, am curious to learn how you obtained this cross." I waited anxiously for a reply, but Lucia simplY shrugged her shoulders and shook her head in ,( disinterested fashion. Then came the manceuvring of a sharp woman, young and full of nerve. "A bargain!" she imperiously ejaculated, yet with • an amount of winning grace. 'Restore the crows, and hereafter—silence. Muro shall leave Yerbe Buena at once." I stood perfectly rigid for a moment, studying her face. There was neither the shadow of doom visible nor the brilliance of triumph —Lucia's extremes under the influence of highly - strung nerves. I handed the cross to her as a soldier • might surrender his sword to a conqueror. She secreted the gift in her bosom, and gave a sigh of relief. "The rascal! " I muttered, shaking my fiat St Muro's retreating form. The comicality of ral bold demeanour, seeing I limped, set Lucia Diet' in a good humour. Gall and wormwood!--the impudent minx burst into a merry ripple of , laughter, and tripped off to join the festive gather' •' in g, whither I dared not follow. Muro would retaliate, that was certain. Plis! he failed to accomplish by open means he would endeavour to carry out by cunning. If Lucia Thal , aided him, she would ipso facto cease to be worth/ of Mark's love. My plain duty was to thwart the plotters, remembering that Burns says truly: "The best laid schemes o' mice an' men Gang aft agley." (To be continued.)
